A Tuskegee Airman’s Legacy of Generational Wealth—and His Granddaughter’s Mission to Expand It

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Julia Lashay Israel bought her first home at 19, never considering it a remarkable achievement.

Only decades later did she begin tracing that sense of possibility to a VA mortgage her grandfather, one of the original Tuskegee Airmen, secured after World War II.

"We never talked about the house. We always talked about the Tuskegee Airmen," Israel tells Realtor.com®.

Her family's experience stands apart from that of many Black Americans. Just 44.4% of Black households owned their homes in the third quarter of 2026, compared with 75.1% of non-Hispanic white households, according to Realtor.com research.

Israel, now a real estate broker and educator, has spent years teaching agents about the discriminatory housing policies that helped create that divide—and how their own practices can perpetuate it.

Ahead of Veterans Day, she shared her grandfather's story at the National Association of Realtors® NXT conference in New Orleans, illustrating what access to homeownership could make possible for generations of a family.

Israel's grandfather Sgt. Morrissey Whitley was just 20 when he entered the military aviation training program in Tuskegee, AL, in 1942—eventually serving in intelligence as a draftsman for the 332nd Fighter Group, according to Israel.

Just a year earlier, the U.S. Army Air Corps had begun admitting Black men after decades of exclusion, following pressure from civil rights organizations.

But the training program remained segregated, and the Tuskegee Airmen faced hostility, unequal treatment, and military officials who questioned whether Black servicemen were capable of flying combat missions.

Despite those systemic obstacles, the Tuskegee Airmen distinguished themselves in combat, helping challenge the racist assumptions that had excluded Black Americans from military aviation.

Whitley made sure his descendants understood their achievements, too.

When Israel told him an elementary school teacher had claimed Black Americans hadn't served in WWII, he assembled a folder of evidence for her to take to class.

But the racial barriers facing Black servicemen didn't end when they returned home.

And in the housing market, those divisions were reinforced by redlining, which restricted where Black families could obtain mortgages—and, ultimately, where they could build wealth.

In 1940, just 22.8% of Black households owned their homes, compared with 45.6% of white households, according to historical U.S. Census Bureau data.

And even while the GI Bill subsequently helped millions of returning veterans purchase homes through government-backed mortgages, lenders often refused to finance properties in predominantly Black neighborhoods.

On top of that, racially restrictive covenants excluded Black buyers from many white neighborhoods.

As a result, just 5.1% of VA-guaranteed loans in an early federal mortgage sample went to Black borrowers, according to a 2026 study published in Nature.

Whitley was among those who secured financing, purchasing a home in Gary, IN, after moving his family from Chicago, according to Israel—a move that she believes was made necessary because of discriminatory lending practices.

"You can't use your VA loan in Chicago," she says. "You have to live where Black people are redlined to live."

During the COVID-19 pandemic, Israel developed a course teaching real estate agents about the history of housing discrimination—including redlining, restrictive covenants, and the industry's role in perpetuating segregation.

She later taught it through Keller Williams' social equity task force, established after George Floyd's killing in 2020. Her training has since reached more than 40,000 agents, according to NAR.

What surprised Israel was how many agents were unfamiliar with that history—or how their own decisions could perpetuate its consequences.

"There's been different buyers, different sellers," she says of the persistence of residential segregation. "The only common denominator is the Realtor®."

Israel has witnessed discrimination firsthand.

"I've had people not sit in closings with us because we were Black," she says, recalling a Minnesota home closing in the mid-2000s.

Black applicants were 7.8 percentage points more likely than white applicants to be denied mortgages, even after accounting for financial characteristics and lender differences, according to a 2026 Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis study.

Israel also worries about agents discouraging buyers from pursuing certain financing programs or directing them toward particular neighborhoods without considering the long-term consequences.

Her courses emphasize the importance of understanding those barriers and earning buyers' trust, particularly when they're navigating a process nobody in their family has experienced before.

During 13 years working primarily with first-time buyers, many of them Black single women, she encountered clients who were hesitant to sign agreements or trust the professionals guiding them.

"A lot of times they are the first, and so if you're the first person who's ever done it, yeah, that's pretty scary," she says.

Israel grew up with a very different understanding of homeownership.

"My grandparents always had a house. My parents always had a house," she says.

In fact, she remembers envying classmates who lived in apartment complexes, where they could play together after school.

Her own children inherited that familiarity. Israel recalls her young son accompanying his grandmother to look at a rental apartment and repeatedly asking, "When's the closing?"

He couldn't understand why someone would pay to live in a home they didn't own.

It wasn't until Israel began working in real estate that she recognized how her family's experience differed from that of many neighbors and fellow churchgoers who rented their homes.

That early exposure can have lasting consequences. Children who grew up entirely in homeowner households were 18.4 percentage points more likely to become homeowners themselves by age 35 than those raised entirely in renter households, according to Realtor.com research.

Housing-related financial advantages can also persist across generations independently of what children earn themselves. Less than half of the persistence in housing capital across generations is explained by children's earnings, according to a 2025 U.S. Census Bureau study examining more than 3.4 million families.

Those advantages have been distributed unequally. Median white family wealth stood at approximately $332,100 in 2025, compared with just $36,800 for Black families, according to a Tax Policy Center analysis of Federal Reserve data.

Israel's own family never had substantial financial resources growing up, despite their history of homeownership.

"We never had a lot of money, but we always had a lot of sense," she recalls.

And when her granddaughter was born, Israel began reconsidering how far the opportunity her grandfather secured had carried the family.

"He didn't leave us any money, but he left us, like, a legacy," she says.

It was that realization that prompted Israel to begin incorporating Whitley's experience into her educational work.

"It's really not about quartz countertops or, you know, the cute fence," she says of the work real estate agents do. "It really is that we're planting seeds for families."

Her own grandfather had planted one more than eight decades earlier. And now, another generation of his descendants is benefiting from the opportunities that followed.

"This little baby is a trust fund baby, and she'll never know that it was because her great-great-grandfather had an opportunity that other people didn't have that set the path of this legacy for her," Israel says.

Allaire Conte is a senior advice writer covering real estate and personal finance trends. She previously served as deputy editor of home services at CNN Underscored Money and was a lead writer at Orchard, where she simplified complex real estate topics for everyday readers. She holds an MFA in Nonfiction Writing from Columbia University and a BFA in Writing, Literature, and Publishing from Emerson College. When she’s not writing about homeownership hurdles and housing market shifts, she’s biking around Brooklyn or baking cakes for her friends.

« La meilleure équipe d'Europe, c'est nous » : Marc Cucurella s'enflamme pour le Real Madrid !

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

En pleine confiance, Marc Cucurella a profité de son passage sur les ondes espagnoles pour désigner le Real Madrid comme la meilleure équipe du continent. Le latéral gauche estime en effet que la formation madrilène propose actuellement le meilleur football en Europe, balayant au passage les récentes critiques subies par le club.

Invité au micro de Juanma Castaño dans l'émission nocturne El Partidazo de COPE, diffusée sur la station Cadena COPE dans la nuit du 9 au 10 novembre 2026, Marc Cucurella a placé le club madrilène comme la meilleure équipe d'Europe. Le joueur estime que la formation espagnole propose actuellement le meilleur football, au-dessus de n'importe quel rival, alors même que le club était la cible de nombreuses critiques quelques semaines auparavant. Une nette montée en puissance. Il faut dire que le Real Madrid reste désormais sur une série impressionnante de huit victoires consécutives toutes compétitions confondues.

Dans ce même entretien, l'international espagnol a également pris la défense de son coéquipier Vinícius Júnior face aux critiques. D'ailleurs, Marc Cucurella a tenu à mettre en avant Arda Güler, qu'il considère comme le joueur madrilène le plus impressionnant cette saison.

Interrogé sur la concurrence européenne et plus particulièrement sur le statut du PSG, le défenseur a placé sans hésiter le Real Madrid devant le club français. Tout en reconnaissant la solidité du projet et la qualité évidente de l'effectif du PSG, il voit l'équipe madrilène un cran au-dessus en ce moment. Une hiérarchie assumée. Toutefois, le joueur a reconnu avec franchise ne pas regarder les matches du club parisien ni son duel face au FC Barcelone, par manque de temps et pour s'occuper de ses enfants.

Une vie de famille bien remplie qui l'éloigne des matches à la télévision, mais qui ne l'empêche pas de maintenir une analyse tranchée sur le rapport de force actuel. Ainsi, malgré les louanges adressées au club de la capitale française, le joueur maintient fermement sa position en faveur du Real Madrid.

Régulièrement aligné avec l'équipe d'Espagne, le latéral gauche prépare un rendez-vous important en Ligue des nations. La Roja disputera en effet la cinquième journée de la compétition le jeudi 12 novembre 2026 sur le terrain de la République tchèque.

Après cette parenthèse internationale, le Real Madrid retrouvera le championnat. Le prochain rendez-vous en club du Real Madrid aura lieu le samedi 21 novembre 2026 au stade Santiago-Bernabéu face au Celta Vigo, à l'occasion d'une nouvelle journée de Liga.

Nottingham Forest plot move for 'colossal' Birmingham City ace

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Birmingham City's ascent to the Championship summit has led to a fresh round of transfer speculation involving German centre-back, Phil Neumann.

According to Sky Deutschland, Nottingham Forest and Fiorentina are casting eyes over the centre-back's strong performances at St. Andrew's @ Knighthead Park.

Blues currently top the table on goal difference, ahead of Middlesbrough and Stoke City with 29 points from their first 15 matches, conceding just 14 goals - the Championship's strongest defensive record.

Neumann, unsurprisingly, has been a key aspect of such an improvement in this part of the West Midlands, having endured an indifferent first season in English football after arriving at Birmingham last summer following a pre-contract agreement during his final months with Hannover 96.

Transfer speculation involving the 29-year-old isn't exactly new at this stage, as the 6'3" figure was the subject of reported interest in the previous January window and then again in the summer.

However, it would be an untimely blow for Chris Davies if Neumann was to depart B9 in the coming months as the ambitious club stand a chance of realising their promotion dream.

Neumann has already surpassed the 50-appearance mark for Birmingham, with the imperious defender being an ever-present alongside Christoph Klarer in the early weeks of the season.

Having scored three times in all competitions last season, his brace against Wrexham on August 28th sent Davies' side on their way to the first of eight victories so far, and he has continued to impress defensively.

Registering 181 defensive contributions thus far, as well as a duel success rate of 60.9%, such performance levels have attracted the attention of Forest, who have conceded 13 goals in their first 10 league games under Oliver Glasner and sit 16th in the Premier League.

Birmingham City and Middlesbrough are both plotting shock moves for Leeds United-linked Lorient star Arthur Avom, Football League World understands

Having been linked with Sassuolo in January, the defender has also been monitored by Fiorentina, who are currently 15th in Serie A after 11 games, despite conceding 25 goals in the process.

It has also been claimed that a deal could be struck as early as January for the player, who has never featured at the top level in any of Europe's major footballing nations.

Neumann was recently described as "colossal" by former EFL striker, Sam Parkin, and Birmingham fans will be hoping the defender stays true to his previous words during the summer transfer window.

Indeed, the former German youth international was linked with Hull City and Borussia Mönchengladbach, although a move to either club failed to materialise.

Neumann also distanced himself from such transfer calls, saying: "It’s not on me, my agent is getting these calls but I don’t care.

"My focus is on Birmingham and to do good in the season," he added.

Given his importance to this promotion-chasing Blues side, many supporters have voiced their immediate thoughts on the speculation surrounding Neumann.

With figures of between €5m and €10m mooted, one wrote: "Firstly, he won’t leave this winter, we’re in a promotion push. Secondly you’ve massively undervalued him. Minimum £18 million."

Another Blues fan added: €5m euros. Might send him out on loan for a couple of games for that."

"One of the best defenders in the league, he shouldn't go for anything less than 15m and hopefully if we're up for promotion he'd stay till the end of the season," was another viewpoint adding to the consensus.

This stance was hammered home by the comment: "If anyone thinks Wagner would take £10m I think they are very much mistaken. Put another 5 on and they may get to have a conversation."

"Some crazy figures being quoted in the responses here. Would agree if he was 5 years younger. Would be surprised if he went for anymore than £8m-£10m. Don’t think we need to sell tbh," was another strong reaction.

Some associated with Forest are also not willing to entertain such speculation, with a Reds fan posting: "Utter rubbish. Brum won't sell and he does not get ahead of Cunha, Murillo, Diomande or Milenkovic."

Neumann's contract at St. Andrew's is said to expire in June 2028, meaning in January, the defender will be entering the final 18 months of his deal.

As such, Birmingham will still hold plenty of power when it comes to negotiations, although it remains to be seen whether either Forest or Fiorentina are to press forward with such advances between now and then.

Only one goal but three points for the Boars: Little Common 0 – 1 Roffey

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Roffey made the long journey to Little Common determined to bounce back from the unlucky defeat against East Grinstead. Pat Gibbs was rested with a niggle after his first start back for months in the previous game, Hayden Skerry is still working back to fitness and the Pearse brothers still missing through injury.

Right from the start Roffey dominated possession and the hosts sat in a low block. Callum Kilkenny had the first attempt on goal when taking a return pass from Jordan Mase before shooting over the bar. Shortly after Kilkenny got round the back and Common keeper Matt Cruttwell had to block his effort with the rebound being scrambled clear. After nine minutes Roffey thought they had a penalty for a foul on Stan Berry but after consulting his assistant, the referee ruled it out.

Chesworth showed a wonderful bit of skill but his cross to unmarked Kilkenny was just intercepted. Next, Jack Meeres came out of the defence to play a one two, but Cruttwell saved his shot. The home side forced a free kick which was headed clear with the return cross coming to nothing. Ten minutes before half time Tom Caplin and Kilkenny combined but the dangerous cross was blocked at the near post. A corner routine led to Charlie Gibson firing in a shot that brought an excellent save from Cruttwell. The ball was cleared long, but any hope of a break was snuffed out by Teddy Wood and Kilkenny.

Thirty-eight minutes and Harrison broke down the right and cut into the area, his cross shot bringing another good save from Cruttwell. Roffey’s pressure intensified as half time approached. Berry brought another fine save from Cruttwell and Mase’s follow up was blocked a couple of yards out. The deadlock was finally broken after a slick passing combination with Chesworth and Mase, released Harrison into the box and he slid the ball inside the far post. The Boars nearly doubled the lead a minute later, but Berry’s effort was scrambled off the line.

Five minutes into the second half a flowing move down the left ended with Berry driving the ball narrowly wide into the side netting. A minute later Kilkenny had Cruttwell diving low to his right to save again. Fifty-six minutes and Mase robbed a defender but shot straight at Cruttwell. On seventy minutes Mase hit a free kick narrowly over the bar as Roffey kept the game in the Common half. Entering the last quarter of the match. Mase hit a swerving drive that brought another great save from Cruttwell. Then the keeper made a more straightforward save from a Caplin effort.

Into the last ten minutes and despite the Boars’ dominance, the lead was still only one and Roffey continued to search for the second. Mase drove over the bar then took the ball off a defender and brought yet another excellent save from Cruttwell, this time low to his left. Wood tried his luck from twenty-five yards, but the home stopper was there again. The mist was now beginning to drift over the pitch as Bailey Moyo shot high and wide. As we entered the last couple of minutes it was still all Roffey, and Wood had his shot deflected into the side netting after a free kick with Jack Meeres’ header from the resulting corner being the last significant action of the match.

Roffey took the three points against a dogged Common side backed up by some outstanding goalkeeping. Despite only scoring one goal, it was a thoroughly professional performance with the home side being denied a shot on goal throughout the ninety minutes. The Boars move back up into the play off-places and now have a ten day break before their next match v Crawley Down Gatwick at the Fusion Aviation Stadium on November 17th. At present Horsham have no game that night so it would be great to see the Lardy Army and regular Horsham supporters there, with concessions for Horsham season ticket holders. The boars are certainly playing a very entertaining brand of football.

Opinion: Liverpool concerns prove that exciting star is on his way to Anfield

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Liverpool haven't been at their best this season.

Whether that's because their players have failed to meet expectations or if its because they've just not got enough shots off, Andoni Iraola's men just haven't been at the races this term.

They've lost a fair few games across all competitions and, without some serious reinforcement in the upcoming transfer windows, they're going to fall even further behind their title rivals.

Obviously, a team of Liverpool's stature should be challenging for silverware every season but, at this rate, that's almost certainly not going to happen.

But, with how many players the Reds have been linked with recently, it's good news to see that some of those deals could now end up being confirmed.

Liverpool have concerns over Milos Kerkez's recent form

Milos Kerkez has probably been one of Liverpool's worst performers this season.

The Hungarian has failed to meet expectations on numerous occasions and, even though there have been times when his talent has shone through, it has often felt like the Reds made the wrong choice when they sold Andy Robertson in the summer.

Indeed, Kostas Tsimikas is another option that the Anfield outfit have but, somehow, he's been even worse than Kerkez.

These poor performances have led to fans questioning whether a new left-back should be on top of Liverpool's shortlist or not.

And now, thanks to a fresh update from Football Insider, it's clear that Liverpool are also thinking the same way.

"Liverpool are beginning to develop concerns about Milos Kerkez's displays so far this season, sources have told Football Insider," they wrote.

"The 22-year-old has retained his position as the starting left-back this season, and has started ten games across all competitions so far, including eight in the Premier League.

"However, his inconsistent displays have led to question marks about his long-term role in the side, and whether Liverpool will need to strengthen in his position.

"Andoni Iraola is a big fan of the Hungary international, who he also worked with at Bournemouth, and has been backed to get him back to his best in the near future."

Liverpool's concerns over Milos Kerkez could lead to them signing Alejandro Balde in January

Several left-backs have been linked with a Liverpool move this season.

Although none of them ended up joining in the summer, many rumours have emerged linking Liverpool with a January move for one or two players.

Among those linked, Barcelona's Alejandro Balde has been a well recieved move by fans.

The 23-year-old was Hansi Flick's starting left-back last term but, for one reason or another, he's been left on the bench this term.

Balde's natural position is at left-back but, during the start of his Barcelona career, he also played a handful of games at right-back.

The La Liga giants only want around £34 million for Balde so a deal for him would be cheap and, since he's already proven that he can handle the Champions League and can contribute to the team well, it feels like a deal for the Spaniard is a no-brainer.

That is, especially if there are concerns about the future of Kerkez in L4.

Hedra Launches Model Factory to Train, Deploy, and Improve Real-World AI

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Hedra is launching Model Factory, a platform that brings GPU infrastructure, model post-training, inference optimization, and production feedback into one workflow. The a16z-backed research company is targeting robotics, manufacturing, biology, and defense teams that need models adapted to their own data and operating conditions.

The pitch addresses a practical gap in enterprise AI: a capable general-purpose model is often only the starting point. Turning it into a reliable system for video, sensor data, or physical actions can require custom training environments, specialized deployment software, and a way to evaluate each new version against the task it must perform.

Model Factory combines flexible GPU sandboxes with post-training performed by Hedra researchers working alongside autonomous AI agents. Customers bring data and simulation engines; Hedra helps construct reinforcement learning environments and datasets, then optimizes how the resulting models run.

“Recursive self-improvement is already changing how frontier models get built,” said Michael Lingelbach, founder and CEO of Hedra. “The next step is the real world, where the data is video, sensors and simulation. Hedra Model Factory gives robotics, manufacturing, biology and defense teams the infrastructure to post-train, deploy and keep improving their own models.”

After deployment, researchers analyze production performance, retrain the model, and release versions only when they pass the customer’s evaluations. That makes the evaluation criteria central to the service: a faster model still has to satisfy the requirements of the application using it.

Hedra uses recursive self-improvement to describe researchers and autonomous AI scientists iterating on experiments, training runs, and optimization methods. The announced process includes researchers and customer evaluation gates, rather than unrestricted automatic changes to production models.

Hedra’s World Model Runtime, or WMR, provides additional context for the launch. The company describes it as an open-core inference engine for image, video, world, and text models, with support for NVIDIA and AMD GPUs. Its optimizations target diffusion steps, attention across frames, and encoding and decoding operations that can consume substantial GPU time in visual workloads.

For a vision-language-action model, inference produces actions as well as interpreting inputs. In a simulated environment, the model and the environment generating its training experience can both require accelerators. Improving the workflow therefore involves more than selecting a model: it can mean reducing data movement, matching numerical precision to quality requirements, and coordinating training with serving.

The company’s GPU sandbox offering lets teams bring containers, dependencies, and model code into isolated environments while Hedra manages placement, scaling, and hardware recovery. Teams can modify their runtime, run training or evaluation, and export checkpoints and artifacts. Its training platform supports supervised fine-tuning, lightweight LoRA adapters, and reinforcement learning, with checkpoint evaluation before serving.

The launch release highlights Krea 3 image editing as one example of the optimization process. Hedra says it reduced a 2K image edit from about 22 seconds to 3.6 seconds on the same four AMD Instinct MI355X GPUs in under a week. The comparison combines inference optimization with distillation: the baseline used the original bf16 model at 30 sampling steps, while the faster version used a distilled 14-step model.

That distinction matters because the result reflects changes to both the model’s computation and the software executing it. It is a reported result for a particular workload and configuration, rather than a general sixfold acceleration for every model served by the platform.

Hedra’s separate Krea 3 engineering write-up details lower-precision matrix operations, custom attention, fused operations, and GPU-to-GPU transfers moved onto dedicated copy engines. Its 22.9-second-to-3.3-second comparison measures diffusion-transformer time, not full-request latency. Those figures should therefore be read separately from the launch release’s image-editing result.

Hedra also reports retraining H3 for physical consistency. The retrained model scored 34.9 on Google DeepMind’s Physics-IQ benchmark, compared with 30.3 for H3 Max, with what Hedra describes as comparable visual quality. An autonomously distilled version rendered a five-second clip in under three seconds on MI355X GPUs, according to the company.

In another example, a simulation study replaced the gripper on a robot running a trained policy. Hedra says matching the replacement gripper’s reach and grasp point in the wrist-camera view restored task success from near zero to near 100% without retraining. The finding concerns the reported simulation setup; it does not establish that arbitrary hardware changes can be handled without further training in deployed robots.

These examples point to different parts of the same engineering problem: preserving useful behavior while changing how a model runs or the system in which it operates.

Model Factory is available starting November 10. Teams can work with Hedra researchers on custom models, start with flexible GPU sandbox capacity without long-term commitments, or bring an existing model to its inference engine. Deployment options include on-premises infrastructure or a nearby partner data center, with optimization targets ranging from faster-than-real-time world action models to vision-language-action models that fit on edge devices.

The company says revenue grew 15 times over the preceding 12 months, although it did not disclose the revenue base in the announcement. Its company overview positions the platform as an extension of experience building and serving its own visual models.

For customers, the substantive proposition is a connected engineering loop: use domain data to improve a model, optimize it for the hardware and latency budget, evaluate the result, and feed production experience into the next iteration. How well that works will depend on the task-specific evaluations and deployment constraints, as much as on the speed of the underlying GPUs.

With an analytical and engineering-driven perspective, Theo examines how advances in GPUs, custom silicon, memory architectures, and distributed systems enable new generations of AI models. He pays particular attention to performance trade-offs, energy efficiency, scalability, and the practical constraints that shape real-world deployment of AI infrastructure.

The Best Jump Starters with Air Compressors, Tested and Reviewed

100+ Most Unique and Practical Gifts 🎁 for Homeowners and DIYers

Published Nov 10, 2026 9:30 AM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

If you’ve ever tried to start your car, but the battery was dead, or you found a tire was too low to drive on, you may have wished for a jump starter air compressor. These combo tools claim to be able to get you going again—so we decided to put 11 of the top models to the test on our own vehicles, and found 7 that we can recommend. DeWalt’s 1600A Jump Starter with Air Compressor took top honors in our tests, thanks to its quality build, internal alternator tester, and convenient pressure presets for accurate inflation.

These devices bring enough voltage and amperage to bring a dead battery back to life. They can also fill a low tire or pump up a leaky one until the driver can make it to a service station for repairs. Ahead, learn the most important factors to consider when choosing one of these combo units and find out how the following models earned a spot on our lineup of the best jump starters with air compressors.

Bottom line: Jump starters with air compressors are essential tools for emergencies, offering reliable performance and versatility. DeWalt leads the pack with its superior features.

There might be a lot to know about these versatile devices, but shopping for the best jump starter with an air compressor doesn’t have to be complicated. To help make the process even easier, these top-rated products are an excellent starting point. We performed hands-on testing with every model listed below to ensure they can meet the grade.

The DeWalt jump starter/compressor combo performed flawlessly in our hands-on tests, easily earning the title of the best overall model. This unit supplies enough juice to start depleted batteries and has a built-in alternator tester.

We got an alternator reading by connecting it to a vehicle’s battery terminals when the vehicle was idling. Since problems starting a vehicle can be due to a faulty alternator, this feature is a nice perk.

The clamps are large and grip the terminals firmly. We were able to get a reading of the current battery voltage. When we connected the terminals to a fully charged battery, the DeWalt indicated the voltage, which is a good way to see the battery’s current charge.

The compressor measured the existing pressure in the tires. We were able to preselect the amount of air we wanted to add, and the compressor inflated the tires to the exact pressure. This handy feature removes the guesswork, especially when inflating bicycle tires that can explode if too much pressure is applied. It took about 20 seconds to inflate bike tires and about 5 minutes to inflate a large pickup tire.

What our tester says: “The DeWalt jump starter was a dream to use—its multifunctionality and high-quality performance made jumping batteries and filling tires almost effortless.”—Glenda Taylor, Product Reviews tester and writer

Get the DeWalt jump starter with air compressor at Tractor Supply Co., Amazon, or The Home Depot.

Jump starters with air compressors can be incredibly versatile, and this model from DieHard definitely fits the bill. The lead-acid jump starter offers 950 peak amps for cranking over large diesel engines as well as reverse hookup protection to avoid damaging the expensive electrical components that are sometimes vulnerable during jumping. It also features two 12V ports and a USB port for charging devices.

The compressor produces up to 150 PSI of pressure and includes a digital gauge for presetting the pressure and checking the battery’s charge level. A built-in work light makes working on the side of the road in low light a bit less difficult as well. This DieHard model also includes three air nozzle adapters for filling sports equipment, pool floats, and other items.

The DieHard jump starter was more than capable of firing up an old Land Rover. It was easier to attach than other models as the cables were slightly longer than the compact versions. And just a flip of the switch turned the unit on for the jumping function. We found that the air compressor worked well, pumping up the tire faster than the other full-size models (about 5 minutes faster from 20 PSI to 36 PSI), though the gauge is in a strange place—it’s right behind the positive clamp. Also, the battery gauge on the front of the unit isn’t all that detailed, providing only broad ranges: 70 to 100 percent, 30 to 70 percent, and under 30 percent.

Get the DieHard jump starter with air compressor at Ace Hardware.

The Trekure jump starter fits a lot of energy into a small package, making it a good option for taking along on road trips. We didn’t have any problems jump-starting three different dead batteries, but the clamps are a little on the small side—we would have appreciated larger clamps.

This unit came with some battery charge, but once it was depleted by testing, it took about 6 hours to recharge. We liked its bright LED flashlight, which allowed us to see what we were doing after dark. The flashlight also doubles as a yellow flashing road flare that can be seen from a distance.

The inflator aired up our bicycle and car tires but didn’t have quite the oomph to fill up larger truck tires. But it’s simple to use—the display screen let us choose from images of a car, motorcycle, bike, or basketball for adjusting air pressure.

We like the Trekure for all-purpose 12-volt jump-starting, but we think it’s best suited for light-duty tire inflation. It comes in a handy case and includes two USB ports and a plug-in DC outlet for charging various devices in the car.

Get the Trekure jump starter with air compressor at Amazon.

Folks who value versatility will want to consider the Schumacher SJ1332 rechargeable power station. This device features 1,200A of cranking power, allowing it to start a massive 8-liter gas engine or a 6-liter diesel engine. It also features a 150 PSI air compressor with an analog gauge for pumping flat tires and a high-volume air port for filling air mattresses and pool floats.

The Schumacher features two 12-volt (V) charging ports, two standard 120V outlets, and a USB port for charging mobile devices. This allows it to charge several devices or act as an emergency power station if the power goes out in the home. Its cables wrap around the unit and clip onto the base for storage, and the compressor hose stores in a small compartment in the back.

We weren’t big fans of the tire pressure gauge, which was not lit and wasn’t very accurate (a difference of around 8 PSI), and it’s in a weird spot (on the back of the jump pack and obscured by the clamp). But otherwise, the unit had plenty of power to jump the car and it was easy to use. We liked the 120V outlets.

What our tester says: “The Schumacher was relatively easy to use to jump my Land Rover. The light was helpful and allowed me to identify the cables easily at night, and the buttons and controls were fairly intuitive.”—Tom Scalisi, Product Reviews tester and writer

Get the Schumacher jump starter with air compressor at Amazon, Walmart, Blain’s Farm & Fleet, or Northern Tool + Equipment.

For an additional well-rounded jump starter, Stanley’s J5C09 portable power station could fit the bill. This jump starter features a lead-acid battery with up to 10,000A of cranking power for turning over large engines in gasoline and diesel vehicles alike. It also includes a 120 PSI compressor that can fill nearly any tire, with an illuminated analog gauge for monitoring the pressure in real-time.

This unit provides some additional built-in features worth noting. It offers a USB port for charging devices, a standard 12V port, a reverse polarity alarm to indicate improperly installed clamps, and a positionable work light for shedding light on the repair area.

During the test, the one feature that stood out the most was the air compressor. It was able to fill a tire in much less time than the other models (about 4 minutes), and the analog display was very accurate. It’s also easy to set up and use in the dark, plus it features extra-long cables to make reaching battery terminals easier. It also had plenty of power to kick the vehicle’s engine over. The positionable light is a nice touch as it allowed us to position the jump pack carefully without losing our light source. The light is a bit weak, however.

Get the Stanley jump starter with air compressor at MSC or Staples.

If you’re looking for a multi-function jump starter/compressor combo, check out this new model from JackPak. Right out of the box, we were impressed with this unit’s build quality—it’s solid, rugged, and comes with heavy-duty clamps.

Like the DeWalt that earned our best overall award, the JackPak shows how many volts the current vehicle battery has, which is great if there isn’t a voltmeter handy. We wish all the units came with this feature.

It supplied enough juice to jump all three dead batteries and took less than three hours to recharge. The compressor feature inflated our bike, car, and truck tires, but it took nearly 30 minutes to completely fill the truck tire. Still, the JackPak isn’t meant as a replacement for a commercial tire inflator, but it will get the job done when a service station isn’t nearby.

We liked the bright LED light on the JackPak, and it doubles as a powerbank for charging devices. It also features a low-power sleep mode that keeps its internal battery from discharging for up to 36 months.

Get the JackPack jump starter with air compressor at Lowe’s or The Home Depot.

When a battery goes dead and a car is stuck in a parking lot or across town (or the side of the road), there’s a lot on the line. We know that, so we tested the jump starters with air compressors on our own depleted car and truck batteries and to inflate a range of tires.

We tested each product by jumping a dead car, truck, and riding mower batteries. We also used a multimeter to determine the voltage from the jump starters. We tested the jump starters over a 2-week period, connecting each one to the various battery terminals and then attempting to start the vehicle.

We also drained some of the air from bike, car, and truck tires to test the built-in compressors. For the inflation tests, we partially deflated bike, car, and truck tires and then timed how long it took each inflator to return the tires to the recommended pressure. During these inflation tests, we paid close attention to how easily the air chucks connected to the valve stems, whether they created a secure seal, and how well they held up through repeated use. We also considered whether the compressors operated consistently under pressure or showed signs of strain, as well as how intuitive and responsive the controls were throughout the process.

We awarded points based on a rubric: the better a jump starter/inflator did on a test or assessment, the higher the points. The products that excelled were given awards based on their strengths, while those that failed were removed from our lineup.

Carrying a jump starter with an air compressor means drivers can handle emergencies and repairs without calling for help (or when help isn’t available). And who doesn’t enjoy a little self-reliance? These devices include built-in jumper cables to clamp onto the positive and negative terminals of a dead battery. It sounds straightforward, but shopping for one of these devices requires a bit of background and know-how. The following sections will break down the most important considerations to keep in mind while shopping for a battery jumper with air compressor.

Jump starters equipped with air compressors include one of two battery types: lithium-ion and lead-acid.

The best jump starters with air compressors—and all portable jump starters, for that matter—have an onboard battery that provides ample power to start a vehicle or operate the compressor. In most cases, the battery size of these portable chargers is described in milliampere hours (mAh) or amp hours (Ah)—1,000mAh equals 1Ah.

Higher mAh or Ah ratings equals a larger capacity to store power, so higher ratings mean more batteries charged and tires inflated between recharges. Generally speaking, most portable jump starters use batteries between 10,000 and 35,000mAh. Remember that higher capacity batteries are heavy, and get heavier as the storage increases.

The purpose of a jump starter is to provide a vehicle with enough power to turn over and fire up the engine, then allow the vehicle’s alternator to continue charging the battery. The amount of power a jump starter needs to accomplish this task is determined by the engine.

Small gasoline engines, such as four-cylinder engines found in most cars (and progressively larger vehicles) don’t require much power. But larger eight-cylinder engines require a bit more. And, because of the extremely high compression ratios of diesel engines, large models found in heavy-duty pickups, RVs, and power equipment require even more power. In general, 1,000A or more will do the trick.

Many manufacturers today don’t explicitly list the amount of power provided by their chargers. Instead, they discuss the types of engines their chargers can handle. This is only a concern for diesel vehicles, as any jump starter will handle most gasoline engines.

While it might seem that jumper cables are all created equal, modern jump starters offer additional safety features. Today’s models protect a vehicle’s electrical system and the person using the cables from sparking or reverse polarity, which happens when the user connects the clamps to the wrong terminals—something that’s very easy to do in the dark. Reverse polarity can wreak havoc on the sensitive electronics in today’s vehicles, so this is a crucial feature.

These units will detect the reverse polarity or contact between the clamps and prevent the unit from expelling stored energy through them. Often, a small light or audible alarm will alert the user so you can understand what’s occurring and why the jump starter isn’t charging the vehicle.

When choosing the best jump starter with an air compressor, there is some variance in the amount of pounds per square inch (PSI) offered by these models. Most models produce around 100 PSI—more than enough for any road vehicle’s tires. Most vehicle tires require just 30 to 40 PSI.

Some models offer 150 PSI or more, which is as much pressure as a traditional home air compressor. Are they necessary for typical vehicle repairs? No. But it might take these compressors less time to boost up a tire on the side of the road, so they could be worth the consideration and splurge.

Several additional features might make a portable jump starter with an air compressor a bit more attractive to consumers.

Even with all of this background on choosing the best jump starter with air compressor, you might have questions. The following section is a collection of some of the most frequently asked questions about battery jump starters with air compressors, so be sure to check for an answer to your question below.stions about jump starters with air compressors, so be sure to check for an answer to your question below.

Dezeen Awards-shortlisted housing project damaged in fire

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Photography and video by Andorran Police unless otherwise stated

A fire has damaged the La Querola housing development in Andorra, designed by French studio Ateliers Jean Nouvel and Spanish studio Ribas & Ribas Arquitectos.

Located in the valley of Ordino, the project is shortlisted in the housing category of this year’s Dezeen Awards and recently won the awards’ public vote.

According to Andorran Police, officials were warned at 01:45 on 22 September about the fire, which spread to all seven buildings in the uninhabited development, affecting 10,000 square metres of housing.

The cause of the fire remains unknown, reported local newspaper La Veu Lliure. No official estimate of the damage has been released, and a date for reconstruction to commence has not yet been announced.

La Querola was completed in October 2025 but had not been occupied. Ateliers Jean Nouvel and Ribas & Ribas Arquitectos designed the housing to appear as if emerging from the natural landscape, with an angular exterior that evokes stratified, eroded rock.

The housing is made up of seven irregularly shaped blocks embedded into the sloping valley.

“The client’s brief called for a residential development within a landscape of exceptional natural value, a context in which any construction inevitably enters into dialogue with a protected mountain environment,” said Ribas & Ribas Arquitectos when entering the housing into the Dezeen Awards.

“The built volume is modelled as a fragmented, stratified mass, avoiding repetitive or generic gestures in favour of a form that reads as eroded geology.”

Clad in locally sourced slate, the building’s facades feature recessed windows and openings revealing outdoor terraces.

Duplex and triplex apartments were designed to have views of the surrounding valley, with wood lining the interior surfaces to add a sense of warmth.

“Voids and cavities pierce the volume, structuring the relationship between built mass and open territory, and generating terraces that function as habitable extensions of the building’s body,” said Ribas & Ribas Arquitectos.

“Openings are conceived not as conventional windows but as deep incisions and habitable cavities – spaces of varying depth that are occupied by fireplaces, bathtubs and other domestic fixtures, making the thickness of the envelope a place in its own right and allowing the geography to penetrate the deepest corners of the interior.”

Other buildings that have been shortlisted in this year’s Dezeen Awards include an off-grid home in Japan topped with a large metal roof and a renovated house in Japan with walls streaked in clay, soil and metal powders.

The Widening Gap Between AI Policy and Real-World Enforcement

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

AI is being deployed across industries and applications faster than the policies and governance frameworks needed to oversee it can mature. Historically, AI applications were largely governed through existing consumer protection, privacy, and industry-specific regulations, alongside companies’ own internal policies. Governments are now introducing AI-specific requirements, but these remain fragmented and often provide limited guidance on how systems should actually operate. Companies are therefore left to reconcile external requirements with their own risk tolerance, product objectives, and operational realities.

The EU is now actively enforcing parts of the AI Act, while U.S. federal policy explicitly favors a less burdensome national framework, with Executive Order 14365 seeking to limit conflicting state requirements, even as states introduced more than 1,000 AI-related measures in 2025. Each of these authorities describes a different destination, and the work of reconciling them is landing on the companies building and deploying the systems.

But establishing regulatory requirements is only part of the challenge. As AI is deployed across more applications and contexts, companies must translate broad requirements and internal policies into operating controls and individual AI decisions. No regulatory framework can anticipate every application, context, or tradeoff. Organizations therefore need to trace a path from governing requirements and internal policies to system behavior, measurable outcomes, and audit or review. The NIST AI Risk Management Framework takes a similar view by emphasizing ongoing measurement and management rather than treating governance as a standalone compliance exercise. Requirements flow down, and evidence flows back up: regulators and executives may define the desired outcomes, but organizations must demonstrate whether their systems are actually producing them.

This places much of the practical responsibility for AI governance on the organizations deploying these systems.

Regulations and governance frameworks generally define principles, obligations, and desired outcomes. Production systems, however, have to translate those into individual decisions, and that translation is where governance gets hard. A policy can say prevent discrimination, protect users, provide transparency, or allow meaningful human review, yet an operational system has to answer far more specific questions: at what threshold does it act, based on what evidence and with what confidence, when is human review required, what gets logged, and what happens when the system is uncertain?

Regulators already expect those answers. The FTC, DOJ, CFPB, and EEOC said jointly that they will enforce their existing authorities on automated systems, which means someone will eventually ask a company to explain how its system decides.

Consider a platform with a clear policy against harassment. A message containing profanity may be harmless banter between friends, while a seemingly innocuous message may be part of a sustained campaign targeting another user. The system has to determine what context it can reliably use, how much evidence is enough to intervene, and what intervention is appropriate: block the message, warn the sender, escalate for human review, or take no action. Lowering detection thresholds may catch more harmful behavior, but also suppress legitimate conversation. Requiring human review may improve judgment, but delay intervention. None of these decisions is specified by a general policy prohibiting harassment, yet together they determine how that policy is actually enforced.

No policy document settles those tradeoffs in advance, so someone inside the company has to decide them, document them, and revisit them. That person is effectively writing policy whether or not the org chart says so.

If the people who implement a system are effectively the ones writing policy, then responsible AI cannot be treated as a statement of intent, because it has to show up in how the system behaves every day. That means managing how AI is used, measured, and corrected over time, not just approving a policy or passing an initial evaluation. Three ideas matter most in practice, and each follows from the tradeoffs above:

Putting these principles into practice requires governance to be built into how AI systems are designed, deployed, and managed. Four practices are particularly important:

Regulation can define obligations and principles, but companies still have to translate them into systems that make real decisions at scale. That makes internal governance an operating responsibility, not just a compliance exercise. Every company that deploys AI at scale is already writing policy through its thresholds, its review rules, and its escalation paths, and the only open question is whether it does so on purpose.

Regulators will keep defining the outcomes they want, and they will not always agree, so the company has to decide how those expectations become controls and then show with evidence that the controls work. Companies that build clear ownership, measurable controls, auditability, and adaptable infrastructure now will be better positioned as regulation continues to evolve.

I’ve seen this problem from several sides: in cybersecurity environments, building risk models, and now operating AI systems at scale. Across each, the lesson is similar: a framework only works if it can be translated into clear decisions, measured in practice, and improved when reality exposes the gaps.

George Ng is CEO and Co-Founder of GGWP, an AI-powered content moderation and community health platform that helps companies understand and manage user behavior over time. He brings a combination of deep technical expertise and applied behavioral science to the challenge of online safety, with a career spanning AI, cybersecurity, and data science across both the private sector and government. Before GGWP, George co-founded Cyence, a cyber risk modeling company that was acquired by Guidewire Software for $275 million. He previously served as Chief Data Scientist at Cray Supercomputer and as a research scientist at DARPA and US-CERT, where he built terrorism models and developed cyber threat signatures. He has taught machine learning, behavioral economics, and game theory at UC Berkeley, UC Irvine, and American University. George holds a Ph.D. in Economics from UC Irvine and a B.A. in Economics from UC Berkeley.

In a revealing sit-down with FourFourTwo last year, USMNT head coach Mauricio Pochettino has finally lifted the lid on a legendary encounter with Sir Alex Ferguson, a meeting that has fuelled a decade of what-ifs at Old Trafford.

The Argentine, who took the reigns of the US national side on a contract running through until 2030, recounted how the most successful manager in British history once sought him out.

“Through the secretary of the League Managers Association, Sir Alex asked for my phone number so that we could have lunch," Pochettino told FourFourTwo.

"It was a meeting in London between colleagues, between a genius and an apprentice.”

“When we asked for the bill, I wanted to take it, and so did he. We started arguing over who’d pay!

Bitcoin Price Trades Down Near $73,500 After Hot CPI Print Lifts Fed Hike Bets

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Bitcoin price slipped to $73,500 Tuesday morning after October consumer prices rose 0.4%, topping forecasts and pushing traders toward a bigger Federal Reserve rate hike in December.

Bitcoin price traded near $73,500 on Tuesday morning, down about 1% over the past day and roughly $32,000 below its price one year ago, after a hotter-than-expected inflation report revived fears of a larger Federal Reserve rate hike, according to Bitcoin Magazine Pro data.

The U.S. consumer price index rose 0.4% in October, above the 0.3% economists expected. Core CPI, which strips out food and energy, climbed 0.3% against forecasts for 0.2%.

Bitcoin price had been holding near $74,300 in the minutes before the 8:30 a.m. ET release. It dropped to about $73,400 right after the print, briefly undercutting Monday’s overnight low of $73,477, before steadying in the high-$73,000s.

JUST IN: U.S. CPI inflation comes in at 0.4% MoM, higher than expectations 👀Bitcoin falls below $74,000 on the news. pic.twitter.com/Zxq3LtVwAd

Food prices jumped 0.5% on the month, energy rose 0.7% and gasoline gained 1.0%. Housing costs increased 0.3%.

The report will not carry an annual inflation rate. The government shutdown in October 2025 kept the Bureau of Labor Statistics from collecting a full set of prices, so there is no comparable figure. Bank of America estimated before the release that the annual rates were running near 3.8% for headline and 2.7% for core.

Bond traders moved first. Two- to five-year Treasury yields rose at least 10 basis points on the day after the print, and traders boosted bets on a half-point hike when the Fed meets on Dec. 9.

That was the scenario Fed Governor Christopher Waller laid out hours earlier. He said he is “leaning toward” a December increase and would back “more robust action” if inflation ran hotter than expected.

“In that case, I believe a 50 basis point hike in the policy rate should be considered at the December meeting,” Waller said. “Hiking only 25 basis points would put our credibility at risk, a risk I am not eager to take.”

Before the data, markets had already largely priced in a quarter-point move. Higher rates raise the cost of holding an asset that pays no yield, and that math has capped the bitcoin price since Friday’s strong jobs report.

Bitcoin had already been soft overnight. It lost about 1.4% from midnight UTC before the data, even as Asian markets rallied. South Korea’s Kospi jumped 4.39% and Japan’s Nikkei 225 rose 2.87%, while oil kept falling after President Donald Trump called fresh strikes on Iran “highly unlikely.”

Stock futures were mixed going into the release. Dow futures rose 156 points, or 0.3%, while Nasdaq-100 futures slipped 0.4% after Monday’s rally gave the S&P 500 its best day since April 8.

Commodities slipped too. West Texas Intermediate crude traded near $84 a barrel, its lowest since late August, and gold fell just over 1% to below $4,100 an ounce.

Fund demand has not cushioned the drop. U.S. spot bitcoin ETFs shed $201 million on Monday, led by $213.2 million leaving BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT), its seventh straight session of redemptions. The streak comes even as BlackRock says demand to swap coins into IBIT in kind is at a record.

The bitcoin price has drifted lower since peaking above $87,000 in early October, as AI-related jitters weighed on Wall Street and ETF buyers stepped back.

Ether fell about 1.4% to near $2,100 overnight, as U.S. spot ether ETFs lost $184.6 million on Monday.

Analysts flagged $73,000 as the line to watch. A daily close below it could open the way to $70,000, while reclaiming $80,000 would point to stronger demand.

Producer price data lands Friday, followed by minutes from the Fed’s October meeting on Nov. 18. Both will help decide whether traders hold onto bets on a half-point hike or pare them back.

At the time of writing, the bitcoin price is near $73,500.

Intel and Altera Develop Modular Sensor Bridges for Physical AI

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Intel and Altera announced a collaboration on November 10, 2026, to develop modular, real-time sensor-bridge reference designs for edge and physical AI systems. The work targets advanced healthcare equipment, aerospace and defense mission systems, and robotics.

The proposed designs combine Intel’s compute, connectivity and software with Altera’s FPGAs, programmable chips intended to adapt as sensors and AI models change. They are aimed at original equipment manufacturers (OEMs) building sensor-rich systems. At this stage, the companies are describing reference solutions under development rather than a finished commercial product.

Edge and physical AI systems can need to consolidate information from sensors using different interfaces while satisfying timing, performance, power and cost constraints. Some applications also require very high bandwidth or place input/output connections away from the compute board. In those designs, sensor data must move rapidly into memory so a host Intel processor can use it for edge, robotics and other workloads.

The modular approach pairs Altera’s programmable hardware with a consistent Intel compute and software foundation. Dan Rodriguez, corporate vice president and general manager of Intel’s Edge Computing Group, said the goal is to preserve OEM flexibility as applications, sensors and workloads evolve, simplify integration, and help customers move from prototype to deployment while meeting performance, power and cost requirements.

The companies intend to make the resulting reference solutions accessible through Intel’s Open Edge Platform SDK and an Altera SDK, according to Altera’s account of the announcement. That establishes the intended software route while the reference designs remain in development.

Intel presents its Open Edge Platform as an open, partner-enabled software base for accelerating edge solution development. Its documented stack includes composable open-source tools, libraries and microservices, along with edge inference support for vision, generative AI and physical AI workloads on Intel silicon.

The platform documentation also lists Robotics AI, Health and Life Science AI, and Federal and Aerospace AI suites. Those existing suites overlap with the collaboration’s target markets, but the announcement does not establish that every suite component is part of the new sensor bridge.

For healthcare, the companies are jointly developing a reference solution for the stringent latency and real-time demands of advanced equipment. The aerospace and defense work focuses on next-generation mission systems, particularly size, weight and power constraints for workloads that converge AI and signal processing.

Robotics would combine Intel compute, connectivity and robotics software with Altera FPGA technology to address sensing, timing and real-time processing in intelligent machines. The three efforts therefore share an architectural approach while addressing different system requirements across healthcare, mission computing and machine control.

Farhad Shafai, Altera’s head of business solutions and marketing, framed the hardware role around physical machines’ need for control. “Physical AI systems have to sense, decide, and act in real time, and the physical world demands accuracy, reliability, and safety,” he said. Shafai said the FPGAs provide sensor connectivity, data processing and deterministic control while remaining programmable as sensors and AI models evolve.

The collaboration’s practical significance is the attempt to pair a consistent Intel compute and software base with programmable sensor connectivity from Altera. For OEMs, the open question is whether the planned reference designs and SDK access can reduce integration work enough to shorten the path from prototype to deployment while satisfying each target market’s system constraints.

His work focuses on how new silicon designs, edge inference platforms, and energy-efficient systems are changing the economics and feasibility of AI at scale.

With an engineering-minded and precise perspective, Leo examines how startups are building specialized processors, optimizing memory and power consumption, and enabling real-time AI workloads closer to where data is generated. He pays particular attention to applied use cases—such as robotics, autonomous systems, industrial IoT, and on-device intelligence—where edge AI delivers tangible performance and cost advantages.

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Home » Blitz news » XRP Price Tests Support After Sharp Slide From $1.32

XRP hits the same structural support pattern tested numerous times before: here’s where the bounce could take us.

XRP has fallen back to roughly $1.16 after failing to hold gains near $1.32 earlier in the week, putting a closely watched support zone in focus.

The token briefly traded above $1.30 amid signs of growing large-holder balances, but the recovery faded as broader selling pressure returned.

The move leaves XRP price near the lower end of a descending channel that has shaped its price action since mid-October.

Several market watchers have identified $1.14 as the immediate line buyers need to defend; a sustained break below it could extend the recent decline.

On four-hour charts, XRP has repeatedly moved between lower support and resistance near $1.30. A hold around $1.14 could open the way for another attempt at the channel’s upper boundary, implying a rebound of about 13% from recent levels.

XRP LOOKS READY TO BOUNCE$XRP has reached the lower boundary of the descending channel that has defined its price action over the past month.If $1.14 holds as support, XRP could rebound toward the channel’s upper boundary at $1.30.That would represent a potential 13% move… https://t.co/HTMbkwJNQJ pic.twitter.com/lUwUT1bQWq

That outcome remains conditional. XRP slipped below $1.20 during the latest pullback, leaving both daily and weekly chart structures under pressure. Resistance is seen first around $1.30, followed by the $1.35 to $1.40 area that capped the earlier advance.

Ripple’s chief technology officer, David Schwartz, addressed criticism tied to the token’s performance as XRP traded near $1.16. He said he has limited influence over its market price, underscoring the distance between Ripple’s corporate developments and day-to-day moves in a publicly traded asset.

Large-wallet accumulation has been one of the more constructive data points. Increasing whale holdings and limited movement among some major XRP addresses is a pattern that some traders interpret as a sign that longer-term holders are not rushing to sell.

But crypto wallet data does not settle the question of whether the rebound can last. Exchange supply, fresh demand and the source of buying matter more than a single technical level, particularly after XRP’s steep retreat from late-September highs.

Google Expands Adobe Integrations Across Drive, Gemini and Search

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Google announced an expanded Adobe partnership at Adobe MAX on November 10, 2026, covering planned Acrobat access from Drive and Gmail, additional Gemini connectors, creative tools in Search’s AI Mode and an exploration of new Photoshop editing capabilities.

The partnership is intended to work in both directions: Adobe’s creative and productivity tools will sit inside Google products, while Google’s frontier AI models will be brought into Adobe apps. Google presented the expansion as a way to avoid the loss of focus caused by switching between apps to edit a PDF or fine-tune an image.

Drive and Gmail will both suggest Acrobat, while file previews will offer an “Edit with” option. Together, those planned entry points create a direct route into Acrobat for PDF work. The features are forthcoming rather than part of a completed rollout.

Adobe will add Acrobat and more creative tools to its connectors in Gemini and Gemini Enterprise. It will also bring creative tools to AI Mode in Google Search, extending the partnership across chat, enterprise and search surfaces.

Businesses will be able to use Adobe’s CX Enterprise Coworker agent inside Gemini Enterprise. Google describes the agent as an AI assistant for designing graphics, working with PDFs and launching marketing campaigns without switching apps. That brings creative, document and campaign tasks into one enterprise workflow.

Adobe and Google DeepMind will also explore new editing capabilities for creative professionals, beginning with an image model for Photoshop. Google says the model could enable near-real-time edits, a conditional possibility rather than a delivered feature or measured performance result.

The announcement builds on an expanded Adobe–Google Cloud partnership announced at Adobe MAX on October 28, 2025. Under that plan, Adobe customers would gain access to Google’s Gemini, Veo and Imagen models in apps including Firefly, Photoshop, Express and Premiere, as well as through GenStudio. Enterprise customers would also be able to customize Google’s models on Vertex AI with proprietary data through Adobe Firefly Foundry. Adobe said customer data used with Google’s models on Vertex AI would not be used to train Google’s foundation models.

Google separately began rolling out new Gemini connected apps on September 23, 2026, with Adobe included in the creativity category. Where that rollout added Adobe to Gemini’s connected-app lineup, the new plan expands those connectors and adds entry points in Drive and Gmail, tools in Search AI Mode and the CX Enterprise Coworker integration.

The expanded partnership now reaches beyond model access into document handling, search, creative work and enterprise agents. Its near-term significance is the number of Google surfaces designated for Adobe workflows, while the integrations remain forthcoming and the Photoshop model stays at the exploration stage.

Stephanie Wrobel on the nuance of twists in thrillers

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Years ago, I wrote about nine types of thriller twists for CrimeReads. I spent so much time thinking about that taxonomy that it found its way into my new novel, Revenge Plot: one of my characters, a bestselling thriller writer, reflects on those twists as she develops her book.

But after writing four thrillers, and reading hundreds more, I’ve realized my taxonomy needs an addendum: not everything you figure out before the author tells you is a twist.

First, a refresher on what a twist it. I define it as a revelation that forces the reader to substantially reinterpret what came before. It is not confirmation of something the reader has been encouraged to suspect, nor the answer to a mystery that the novel has supplied enough evidence for the reader to solve. I would call these reveals.

Let’s use Gone Girl as an example, since it’s arguably the most popular thriller of the twenty-first century. Spoilers ahead! Nick’s affair with Andie is a reveal. Nick’s lies to the police are reveals. The book’s twist is that Amy staged her disappearance, fabricated the diary and evidence, and constructed a murder case against Nick.

A reveal gives the reader new information that answers a question or changes what we know. A twist changes the meaning of what we thought we knew. Gillian Flynn gives us clues that Nick is hiding something, so when we find out he’s having an affair and lying to the police, these developments confirm our suspicions of him. But at the point when we find out the diary is fabricated, we believe Amy to be the victim of this story and Nick to be the perpetrator. Finding out Amy is framing Nick forces us to reinterpret everything that came before.

Okay, so why am I being such a pedant about terminology? Because our genre’s overemphasis on twists is ruining one of the key pleasures of thrillers: deduction.

Lots of readers choose mysteries and suspense novels so they can put the clues together, then solve the crimes themselves. Sometimes we want you to say, Holy shit, what?! But other times we want you to think, I knew it! Or, Oh, of course! A good thriller should make the reader feel smart. Thriller writers plant breadcrumbs over the course of three or four hundred pages in the hope that readers will pick up on some or all of them and put the puzzle pieces together.

Our job is not to keep you from figuring anything out. It’s to control what you figure out, and when.

Alfred Hitchcock famously illustrated the distinction between surprise and suspense with his “bomb under the table” scenario. If two people are talking and a bomb suddenly explodes beneath them, the audience is surprised. Tell the audience beforehand that the bomb is there while leaving the characters oblivious, and the same scene is suspenseful. In other words, sometimes giving the audience more information is the key to making a story work.

Thriller writers constantly make versions of this choice. Sometimes I want the reader to know something my protagonist doesn’t. Sometimes I want you to suspect something but not have enough evidence to be sure. Sometimes I want you and the protagonist to arrive at the answer together. And sometimes I want you to be confidently, spectacularly wrong.

All of those experiences require the author to manage the distance between what the reader knows, what the characters know, and what the reader thinks she knows. If I’ve spent a hundred pages planting clues that Character A is secretly Character B, then you figuring out their identity on page three hundred and fifty may not mean you’ve outsmarted me. It may mean I’ve done my job.

That isn’t to say predictability is never a problem. If I intend to stun you on page three hundred yet you figure out my big twist on page thirty, something went awry. But “I guessed it” doesn’t tell us whether that happened. The more useful question is: were you supposed to be able to guess it? And when?

In our obsession with avoiding predictability, I worry that thriller writers are being pushed toward a different—and in my opinion, worse—problem: the supposedly “unguessable” twist.

Surprise alone is easy. I could reveal on the final page of my novel that a murderer was a previously unmentioned identical twin. You probably wouldn’t have guessed it, but you also might want to chuck my book across the room.

The best twists don’t merely make us say, I never saw that coming. They make us say, How did I not see that coming? Once the truth is revealed, we should be able to look backward and recognize that the clues were there all along. The author showed us the pieces; we assembled the puzzle incorrectly.

Which means “unguessable” is a strange standard to aspire to. So many reviews these days focus on whether the reader guessed the twist. (I’m guilty of this too!) So many blurbs on book covers promise one you’ll never see coming. But when every thriller is judged according to how thoroughly it fooled us, we flatten a genre capable of producing far more complicated pleasures.

We’re supposed to notice the suspicious alibi. Remember the odd detail from chapter three. Wonder why the author chose that particular word. Form theories, discard them, resurrect them fifty pages later. We’re supposed to be right sometimes.

To judge a book’s success by whether you guessed the twist early—never mind whether that “twist” was actually a reveal—is like judging a roller coaster by whether you could see the big drop coming.

Sometimes seeing it coming is half the fun.

Stephanie Wrobel grew up in Chicago but has been living in the UK for the last three years with her husband and dog, Moose Barkwinkle. She has an MFA from Emerson College and has had short fiction published in Bellevue Literary Review. Before turning to fiction, she worked as a creative copywriter at various advertising agencies. Her new book, Darling Rose Gold is now available from Berkley Books.
