On Mole Men and Other Inspirations

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

I’d heard about the mole people living in the tunnels under Manhattan. But I only thought about them a few times a day, tops. Did they all carry flashlights, I wondered, or had their eyes evolved to pierce the perpetual subterranean night? Were rats a delicacy with the right recipe, or more of a utility feed for on-the-go snacking? Did a mole man’s skin become increasingly translucent, like certain shrimp, the deeper you went down?

Some questions will remain forever unanswered. As humans, we’re all piloted by this great mystery inside our heads. That’s something I thought about often while writing my novel Middleman. In an uncertain world, the main character seeks clarity and connection to the real thing, as he struggles to make peace with the never knowing. That sense of the never knowing felt especially vast when I was in my early twenties, twenty years ago, and included countless topics, mole people among them.

And then one night, I was waiting on the A train for a while. Long enough to sit down on a bench, though I had no disinfectant. I occupied myself with my notebook, writing my little things as I do, until I looked up to see if a subway was coming. What I saw instead was the silhouette of a hunched figure in the tunnel moving toward me. The handful of people waiting there all appeared distracted by their iPods and glossy magazines. We’re pretty good as a species at putting the blinders on.

But the woman sitting next to me on the bench did notice. She said, in a horrified whisper, “What is that?”

I had an idea, though I was too spooked to speak. The figure had a slight limp, but then as it entered the dim light of the station, it quickly pulled itself up onto the platform with impressive agility. It was a short man with a tangled gray beard, but most surprising was his attire. He was dressed almost entirely in bright green. Green pointy shoes even and a green felt cap with a little red feather. It sounds too cartoonish to be real, I know, but it’s true. I’d pass a lie detector test telling you that this mole man was dressed like Robin Hood. And not the Kevin Costener version. The original Howard Pyle one, the Disney one, the very green fella.

I was still new to the city then, but I’d learned already what every bumpkin must learn there, which is: you don’t have to engage with everyone, or anyone for that matter. Mind your own business and the person on the street with arrows sticking out of his back will move on, and you can go back to your eggs benedict. There are just too many people all at once, and you have to protect yourself from some of these wildcards, unless you want to suffer a lukewarm breakfast, or worse.

So, I didn’t make eye contact, as the guy got closer. I busied myself counting my shoes—one, and oh, look, there’s another one. This strategy didn’t work. He came right up to me and looked at my notebook. He gave a faint nod. I don’t recall exchanging any pleasantries. I just remember him saying, his voice gravelly, these words exactly: “If you ever run into trouble in the tunnels, tell ‘em you know Hobbit.”

Life is strange. And frightening. And funny. A lot of things can happen out there.

It didn’t occur to me until this very moment that maybe he was supposed to be dressed like a hobbit from L0rd of the Rings, and not Robin Hood, after all. Either way, he was living a cosplay fantasy life in the presumably dangerous underground. And after delivering his message to me and me alone, he turned and went back down the platform, climbed onto the tracks, and vanished again into the subway tunnel. That was it.

The woman next to me on the bench had taken off wisely before he limped up, and so I sat there alone wondering: What kind of unexpected turns would my life have to take to lead me into the tunnels? And who was the them, in Hobbit’s scenario, that might be giving me trouble down there? And how dire and desperate would my situation have to be to have just one card left to play, and it’s: name-dropping a mole man named Hobbit?

Life is strange. And frightening. And funny. A lot of things can happen out there. And this memory of Hobbit returned to me as I was writing the ne’er-do-well character, Eddie, in my book. Eddie doesn’t do any of the things a respectable adult is supposed to do. He doesn’t work, doesn’t have a family, doesn’t properly take care of his sick mother. He’s selfish, unscrupulous, and also, he’s enviably free, in his way.

He doesn’t compromise himself and he has the courage to pursue his own vision, however warped and misguided it may be, into the mausoleums of an old cemetery, a scary place for most people even in daylight. Perhaps almost as scary as the dark tunnels under Manhattan, where Hobbit went to an extreme to turn his fantasy life into his real one.

It feels fitting to me now that someone who lived like that would offer a young aspiring writer his protection, his blessing even maybe. And maybe someday I’ll be brave enough to follow him all the way down.

Brendan Flaherty is from outside Hartford. He went to Washington University in St. Louis, and received his MFA from Boston University. He lives with his wife and two sons. The Dredge is his debut novel.

Commvault Partners with Zscaler to Govern and Recover Enterprise AI Agents

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Commvault announced a strategic partnership with Zscaler on November 10, 2026, combining AI-use discovery and policy enforcement with recovery for data, applications and identities. The companies plan to help enterprises govern production AI agents and roll back harmful or unintended activity.

The partnership addresses the operational gap between controlling an agent and repairing what it changes. Zscaler Security for AI identifies enterprise use of AI applications and enforces policies for users and AI agents. Commvault’s AI and cyber-resilience technology restores data, applications and identities after attacks, disruptions or outages, creating a proposed path from detection through recovery.

That division matters when experimental agents become production systems with direct access to business data. Security and IT teams must determine which AI tools employees and developers use, what data those tools share, which activities to permit or block, and whether unwanted changes can be reversed quickly.

The companies named AI discovery, runtime governance, and agent detection and recovery as use cases for the partnership. The announcement presents these as planned collaboration areas rather than a generally available joint product.

One workstream would expand enterprise-wide discovery and maintain continuous visibility into active AI applications, models and tools. Another would create guardrails that flag unsanctioned AI activity and prevent sensitive-data exposure. The third pairs real-time protection for autonomous agent operations with rapid, clean recovery following a security event or risky AI action.

Alongside the partnership, Commvault provided a more detailed view of its recovery approach in a separate AgentRecover announcement. The new capabilities support Amazon Bedrock AgentCore and Microsoft Foundry, platforms that provide services for building, deploying and operating production agents. AgentRecover is designed to audit agent activity, restore agents and recover affected data and systems across environments.

Commvault Chief Technology and AI Officer Pranay Ahlawat said agents do not behave deterministically and that a single agent decision can trigger changes across data, applications and infrastructure on multiple platforms. Such an unintended action can propagate at machine speed, affecting data integrity, application availability and business operations.

For supported platforms, AgentRecover is designed to discover agents and map the data, applications, identities, configurations and infrastructure with which they interact. That dependency map is intended to reveal protection gaps. Its auditing functions are designed to establish what an agent did and what it may have affected across platforms, helping teams prioritize recovery work.

Recovery is designed to cover both the agent and the affected resources while concentrating on what the agent changed. Customers can also use AgentRecover with Commvault’s identity recovery and change auditing to examine agents’ actions, the data they changed and the identities through which they acted.

Commvault is targeting early access for Microsoft Foundry in November 2026 and for Amazon Bedrock AgentCore in December 2026. Those targets apply to AgentRecover’s rollout; the Zscaler announcement does not make them availability dates for the partnership’s planned work.

The partnership connects preventive controls with a recovery path for changes that slip past them. Its immediate significance is the division of labor: Zscaler would provide visibility and enforcement, while Commvault would address restoration after an incident. Turning those planned use cases into working discovery, governance and agent-recovery capabilities for joint customers is the central execution test.

22 Years Later, The Polar Express' Best Line Is Still One Of The Best Christmas Movie Quotes

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Published in 1985, Chris Van Allsburg's picture book "The Polar Express" is a staple of children's Christmas literature. Filmmaker Robert Zemeckis brought its yuletide magic to the silver screen in 2004 with an animated film. Zemeckis had once blended live-action and animation in "Who Framed Roger Rabbit" by sticking Bob Hoskins next to cartoon characters, and with "Polar Express," he aimed to achieve an even deeper fusing of mediums with motion capture.

"Polar Express" didn't open to the smoothest ride. With an exorbitant $170 million budget, the movie was greeted by some as a failure for grossing only $286 million. The use of motion capture animation made the whole thing look uncanny (and became a "30 Rock" punchline), which is the last thing you want for what's meant to be a heartwarming family movie. Not that this stopped Zemeckis from using the same "Polar Express" style in 2007's "Beowulf" and 2009's "A Christmas Carol" (featuring Jim Carrey as Scrooge). "The Polar Express" divided critics in its day, as seen by its 55% Rotten Tomatoes score. Roger Ebert gave it a perfect four stars rating, but others couldn't get over the creepy animation.

But any kids movie seen by enough kids of its generation usually transforms into a classic with time. The movie is also often re-released in theaters during the holiday season. There have been events like branded Polar Express train trips or "Polar Express in Concert," too. Many see "The Polar Express" as a ride they like to take again and again, and, as of 2024, a sequel was in very early development. Let's take a look at one of the film's most memorable quotes.

"One thing about trains: it doesn't matter where they're going. What matters is deciding to get on."

The Conductor (Tom Hanks) tells this to the Hero Boy (Daryl Sabara, with some motion capture work from Josh Hutcherson) at the end of the latter's adventure. The boy is departing the Polar Express and returning home, never to doubt the existence of Santa Claus again. It's a clear rewording of the "it's the journey, not the destination, that matters" aphorism, but tweaked in a way to fit the story's themes of faith and belief.

Note that, in the film, we see the Conductor makes the kids choose to get onboard. The train shows up in front of their house, he tells them they're going to the North Pole, and if they refuse, he shrugs and says "suit yourself." The Hero Boy only jumps on when the train starts pulling out of his yard, seizing his moment and making his leap of faith at the last chance.

Faith is only faith if you believe without having true proof that the object of your belief is real. Even for something as harmless as choosing to believe in Santa Claus, making a choice of what to do with your life requires faith. Note also the other name for a train: a locomotive, a name all about movement. What matters is deciding to get on the train, the Conductor says, because choosing to not get on means standing in place and surrendering to fear of change. It's a good message to have in mind during Christmas, as one year closes out and a fresh one is about to blow in like a winter storm — or, indeed, a train arriving at a station.

Virginia Trench on psychological thrillers and the dark sides of motherhood

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

There were ear-splitting screams. Bites. Lies of all shapes and sizes. Existential crises. And, it goes without saying, a lot of blood. Motherhood has all the raw materials for a good thriller.

Certainly not me—I was about to get a crash course, though. I was blissfully unaware of just how real my work would become once I had a newborn and second-degree tear in tow. I never expected to bring some of the most visceral aspects of my work home with me. But when my daughter arrived, all that went out the window.

From the moment we left the hospital, both of us exhausted, well out of our depth, and wearing diapers, something felt broken in me. (The baby, to be clear, was absolutely perfect).

I, by contrast, was nothing like the serene, softly smiling, well-lit mothers of Instagram. I felt more like a light-averse creature sharing more in common with feral raccoon than a human woman. I craved everything I couldn’t have: silence, rest, solitude, the home phone number of the anesthesiologist who messed up my epidural. A girl can dream—a mother can just tough it out.

As our new reality set in, everything I’d ever heard about motherhood started to seem like an elaborate, generations-long lie. But then, I reasoned, that couldn’t be the case. Surely, I must be the ugly, defective exception to the rule that childbirth and motherhood are the most beautiful, natural things in the world.

I gritted my teeth and waited for a switch to flip. Not not fantasizing about smashing the rocking swing with the annoying music, I did my best to fake it. I was desperate for the *alleged* biologically hardwired maternal peace to settle over us, to give my daughter the mother she deserved…and to prove the lactation consultant wrong—I wasn’t already failing because I’d given up on breastfeeding pretty much immediately.

Goodbye, Instagram moms. Hello, Nightbitch.

Sleepless night after shapeless day into the next sleepless night, I sank into a quicksand isolation, anger, and panic that came in bone-deep waves. Sure, I’d heard of postpartum depression, courtesy of delicately worded waiting room posters. I’d heard of the “baby blues,” a term was popularized by a man decades before American women could open their own bank accounts, receive paid maternity leave, or easily access contraception.

This experience was something else entirely, something huge, hungry, and raw. I used to love exploring those dark corners of human psychology. Through fiction, it was safe, easy to do from a creative distance. But that distance had vanished, and I found myself submerged in the extreme emotions that fuel stories I had no desire to live firsthand. The more I tried to escape those feelings, to remind myself how lucky I was and of the many privileges I have, the thicker my haze of shame became.

Out of sheer desperation, I tried something that seemed a little crazy at the time, even for a woman who heard phantom baby cries everywhere she went, including an empty Target bathroom. I evicted The Happiest Baby on the Block from my nightstand and replaced it with Rachel Yoder’s Nightbitch.

I faced the scary, socially unacceptable thoughts head-on. I nodded vigorously as I read. I started writing again. I gave voice to the emotional exhaust that followed me around like a swarm of black flies. And little by little, this brought me back from the brink.

In those pages, I finally, finally, found someone willing to level with me about the dark side of mothering.

As I tore through Yoder’s novel, I breathed in the unflinching honesty like fresh air. And after what I can only describe as the most isolated period of my life (despite rarely being alone), I could have lifted up Nightbitch like Simba in the Lion King.

To some, a twisted tale of rage and supernatural transformation might seem like an odd place to look for someone who gets you. Those people probably haven’t had to catch tiny human vomit with their bare hands or scrape diaper material from under their fingernails. For me, it was healing. It was therapy for the price of a hardcover—that eventually led to actual therapy. It was someone else, okay, fine, a fictional character, but still, validating everything so much of the world seems determined to silence.

Nightbitch was the gateway to more dark tales. It became an unexpected path back to a part of myself that got lost somewhere in the process of creating life and nesting the crap out of the former guest room. More than that, once I started reading thrillers again, I did so with new, narrowed eyes.

Psychological thrillers are a powerful channel for female rage, and the mom of it all adds fascinating layers to that rage. It’s hard not to occasionally lose your shit when (almost) everything is your fault.

Freudian psychology, direct ancestor of the manosphere, if you ask me, laid the groundwork to blame mothers for the behaviors and even the crimes of their children—especially their sons. We wonder: if a serial killer had just been nurtured properly, might innocent lives have been spared? Culture-defining classics from Psycho to Carrie feature the consequences of dangerous mothering, and audiences point the proverbial finger accordingly.

For that matter, is there anything more frightening than a bad mother? Sharp Objects and Verity terrify readers not just because of their villains’ psychosis, but their fundamental wrongness, their choice to harm, rather than nurture. That goes against nature, doesn’t it? After all, violence is supposed to be a man’s thing, isn’t it?

Most (all?) maternal attributes are fair game. A demanding career? Lack of career outside the home? An only child? Four under four? Homemade gluten free snacks? A Costco multipack or Cheez-Its?

Nothing is safe. All that pressure is enough to make anyone snap. And in this genre, that’s a great place to start.

Open Best Offer Wins, All the Other Mothers Hate Me, or Little Fires Everywhere, and we see death by a thousand little judgmental cuts, some of which are self-inflicted by the main characters. And as their stories unfold, we can’t look away.

Because instead of simply swallowing the judgement and striving to meet those double standards, these women make all hell break loose. And, as I can attest, there’s something cathartic about seeing that happen on the page. Many mothers have met their version of Renate Kline, for instance, ultra-judgmental supermom of Big Little Lies, and we can’t very well face off in real life and tell her exactly what we think of her, so it’s all the more satisfying to watch Reese Witherspoon do it for us in the TV adaptation.

It’s no wonder this genre draws in so many readers—we can relate to the characters’ experiences, ideally minus the murders.

If a “right” formula for this, it has eluded me completely. Too much hinges on who did/didn’t sleep through the night, which appliances are whistling at me, and whether my three-year-old is in a self-declared Aurora/ Ariel or Maleficent/ Ursula mood. So, I gave up looking, and I’m a much happier, better writer for it.

But I will tell you one thing: it helps to not have photos of my children in my office, and I feel zero guilt about this. Do I have a framed tooth in honor of the twist in Sharp Objects? A black and white portrait of Agatha Christie? Also, yes. But no adorable kids, and that’s okay, especially when I’m drafting a murderous chase scene.

It probably sounds corny: but the literal separation was necessary, even if my home office is feet away from the nursery. The best I can do is give make the space I can for different facets of my identity, which now includes, but is not eclipsed by, motherhood. Sometimes that space feels tiny, like hiding the last Annie’s Bunny Cracker bag for myself to pair with a glass of pinot noir.

Sometimes it feels huge, the inspiration for entire second novel, for instance, in all its raw, unfiltered glory (plus one sociopathic con man). What could go wrong?

Virginia Trench is the author of Our Secrets Were Safe. She studied English at Vanderbilt University before earning an MA in secondary education at University of Colorado Boulder. When she’s not writing, she can be found exploring the Rocky Mountains, struggling on a yoga mat while her cat judges her, or devouring the latest crime novels. She lives in Colorado with her family.

Of mistletoe and murder: 7 holiday mysteries set in the past

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Mysteries set during the weeks leading up to Christmas are some of my favorites, and I know I’m hardly alone in loving a good holiday read. There’s just something about the sights and sounds of merriment juxtaposed with the increasingly dark and cold nights that make for a mystery that is that much more fun than one set in any other time of the year.

This is especially true for historical mysteries, in my opinion. For instance, in Murder of a Merry Gentleman, the fourth installment of my Lady Petra Inquires series set in Regency-era England, I set the mystery of a bachelor gentleman’s death in the ballroom of the famed Almack’s Assembly Rooms during the fortnight (i.e., two weeks) before Christmas.

Possibly helped by the lack of modern technology, sometimes the absence of modern lighting, and occasionally the dangers of period-correct clothing, all the little contradictions that give a mystery with a Christmastime setting such an fascinating ambience made it all the more fun for me to write, and I think makes holiday whodunits set in the past all the more enjoyable to read.

Here are seven historical mysteries I’ve especially loved that are all set during the holidays, where the festive season gives as much to the whodunit as the plot, no matter what the time period.

Setting: New York, 1932.  After Grace Verlander, the assistant who keeps Macy’s department store running smoothly, receives a note from Santa Claus that claims he’s been sent a death threat, she ramps up security around the annual Thanksgiving Day Parade. Yet when a dead body turns up on a parade float, Grace must use all the secrets she knows about the store to help uncover a killer and keep Macy’s reputation shining.

Rhys Bowen, The Twelve Clues of Christmas (Her Royal Spyness, book 6)

Setting: Scotland and England, 1933.  When Lady Georgie escapes the dreadfully cold Scottish winter to help play hostess at a holiday party in the village of Tiddleton-under-Lovey, she doesn’t expect a series of murders to be part of the season. And when they start following the theme of a well-known Christmas carol, it’s up to Georgie to find the culprit before the twelve days turn even more tragic.

Jess Armstrong, The Devil in Oxford (Ruby Vaughn mysteries, book 3)

Setting:  Oxford, England, 1922. American heiress Ruby Vaughn is hoping to spend a quiet Christmas in Oxford when she reluctantly agrees to accompany her octogenarian employer, Mr. Owen, to an antiquities exhibition with a mysterious past.

But when the discredited scholar who collected the artifacts turns up dead, and an old friend of Ruby’s asks for her help, Ruby will be pulled into an investigation into the dark world of antiquities that may turn out to be more dangerous than she ever imagined.

As he splits his time between London and the northern town of Stirrington, where he is running for Parliament, Lenox is drawn into a complex case that mixes politics and murder, bringing death and danger closer to home than ever before.

Honda shows off its next electric motorcycle, and it’s wilder than you thought

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Honda has been getting increasingly serious about electric motorcycles, but if you want to see just how weird the company thinks the future could get, take a look at the Honda EV Outlier Concept.

I got a chance to see the wild-looking machine in person at EICMA in Milan, where it looked like almost nothing else on the show floor. And that’s exactly the point.

The EV Outlier Concept originally debuted at last year’s Japan Mobility Show as Honda’s attempt to imagine an electric motorcycle that isn’t simply a conventional motorcycle with the engine swapped for a battery and motor. In fact, Honda describes the concept as looking toward the future of electric motorcycles beyond 2030.

Instead of a centrally mounted motor driving the rear wheel, the EV Outlier uses motors inside both wheels, giving it all-wheel drive and allowing Honda’s designers to completely rethink the rest of the motorcycle’s layout. The battery is housed in that large central black body, while the rider sits extremely low and behind it.

Honda describes the design philosophy with three words: “Gliding, Ecstasy, and Low.” The first refers to the smooth, nearly silent feeling of electric propulsion, while the second describes the instant acceleration and massive torque available from independently controlled front and rear motors. “Low” is more literal: Honda deliberately dropped the rider’s seating and eye position to create an entirely different sensation from a conventional motorcycle.

There’s even a bucket-style backrest designed to support the rider under hard acceleration and let the rider pivot through corners around their hips.

Up front, traditional mirrors are replaced by cameras, while a wide digital cockpit can display things like lean angle, riding modes, front motor torque adjustment, and what the two wheels are doing in real time.

Will Honda actually build this thing? Almost certainly not exactly as you see it here. That’s what concepts are for.

But the EV Outlier is still interesting as a statement of intent. Honda is obviously still playing around with the freedom that electric drivetrains offer, and the company is asking what motorcycles could become once designers no longer have to work around engines, fuel tanks, transmissions, and traditional drivetrains.

And judging by what I saw at EICMA, Honda’s answer could get pretty wild.

In medieval Italy, a spider bite could send you into a dancing frenzy

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 10, 2026 9:01 AM EST

There’s a chance that if you visited southern Italy during the Middle Ages, you might happen upon a port town where people were dancing uncontrollably in the streets. You’d find them frantically spinning, jumping…even crawling on the ground. But unlike the broader “dancing mania” that was taking place across Europe at the same time, this dance, dubbed the “tarantella,” was more than a sickness. It was also a cure.

“Some people say that the bite of a spider caused people to tremor and shake,” Anna Harsh, a professional dancer and founder of the Allegro Dance Company, which specializes in Italian folk dances, tells Popular Science. They’d then continue moving to sweat out the poison.

Tarantism, a historical psychological and social phenomenon that led to anxiety, melancholia, and uncontrollable movement, ran rapidly throughout southern Italy from the 11th to the 17th century.

The epicenter of tarantism was Taranto, a coastal city in southern Italy’s Puglia region, which occupies the “heel” of the country’s boot shape. Each summer harvest season, local residents would spend long days working in the fields where large, hairy wolf spiders burrowed.

Most people believed that these wolf spiders carried a toxic venom. If they bit you, tarantism would quickly set in. The only cure would be to sweat out the venom through continuous, fast-paced dancing. We’re talking uncontrollable, flailing movements that would eventually exhaust both the dancer and the illness.

Although southern Italy was the epicenter of tarantism, similar outbreaks of involuntary, convulsive dancing occurred throughout the Middle Ages. The first major recorded frenzy began on June 24, 1374 in Aachen, Germany. Soon after, others followed in countries like Switzerland and modern-day France. In many of these cases, those afflicted danced uncontrollably for days or weeks. Some even died dancing. Since these events happened centuries ago, no one really knows what caused them. But the tarantism of southern Italy and its ensuing tarantella dance both seem unique.

The name “tarantula” may have originated with the wolf spiders (Lycosa tarantula) of Taranto, but wolf spiders and tarantulas belong to two entirely different spider families. Still, with a leg span that can reach up to three to four inches across, multiple sets of eyes, and a series of odd, jerky movements when they walk, wolf spiders make easy targets for conspiracy theories and urban legends.

While they can look frightening, science has determined over the years that a wolf spider’s bite is generally harmless to humans. Symptoms appear more like a mild bee sting, with a bit of localized pain and swelling, then a full-blown disco dance party. In fact, among the world’s 38,000 known spider species, only about 100 species have venom that is detrimental to humans.

In 1976, a college paper blamed the Salem witch trials on moldy bread. It wasn’t true.

Ancient Romans thought drinking gladiator blood could cure epilepsy

In medieval France, murderous pigs faced trial and execution

4 times drinking coffee was illegal—or even punishable by death

The mysterious dancing plague that killed people in 1518

The radioactive ‘miracle water’ that killed its believers

In August 1693, an anonymous Neapolitan doctor even tested the connection between the bite of a wolf spider and tarantism. He let two wolf spiders bite him in a public experiment, but the doctor never broke out into fancy footwork.

Instead, modern science shows that the extreme summer heat, physical stress, dehydration, and even ample wine drinking could have easily caused the symptoms of tarantism. Still, the wolf spider and tarantism remain undisputedly linked.

Sometimes called the “Dance of the Spider,” the tarantella began as a ritual healing dance that aimed to sweat out any venom associated with tarantism. Many versions of the tarantella exist today, though they’re more traditional folk dances than medical treatments. Instead, the  ‘tarantella as cure’ dance is more formally known as the pizzica. The word pizzica comes from the Italian verb pizzicare, which means “to pinch” or “to bite.”

“Today we might think of that ‘bite’ as something internal, like stress or anxiety,” says Harsh. Picture when someone you know suddenly loses a loved one and they’re shaking uncontrollably. “That person will dance or move and do whatever they can to help them get through that situation.”

Pizzica tarantata is performed almost like an exorcism, the spiritual practice of evicting demons or malevolent entities (such as supposed spider’s venom) through a sort of ritual—in this case, ecstatic, free-form dance.

Musicians gathered around the inflicted victim, playing incessant, fast-paced 6/8-tempo rhythms driven by a tambourine, violins, and guitars. The person suffering from tarantism would then “go into a trance,” says Harsh. “There was shaking, hopping, swirling, and turning. Some people would roll on the ground and come back up. The pizzica has movements, but you can put them in any order. It’s however the spirit moves you.”

These days, most historians and scientists agree that wolf spiders didn’t cause tarantism. Rather, tarantism was a form of mass hysteria, a situation where a group of people share the same intense fear, anxiety, or physical sickness—like dizziness, shaking, or stomach aches—even though there is no real physical or medical grounds for it.

Tarantism mostly afflicted impoverished individuals, particularly women (tarantatas). One possible reason: “Women were oppressed and faced plenty of inequality,” says Harsh. “Dance is a way that they could express themselves, even scream, through movement. That they could be the center of attention for just a minute and be seen.”

Over the centuries, the tarantella has become a celebration of life and joy rather than a frantic ritual. Italians often perform this now-beloved folk dance at weddings and festivals.

Every August, La Notte della Taranta, Europe’s largest open-air festival dedicated to traditional folk music, the pizzica, and the tarantella, takes place in Italy’s Puglia region.

Whether or not dancing works to sweat out a toxic venom, studies do show that dancing helps reduce stress and increases levels of serotonin, a hormone that helps regulate mood, sleep, and digestion.

“You know, whatever is biting at us, whether it’s grief or loss, we can move together, follow the rhythm of a drummer, and heal ourselves in a sense,” says Harsh. “The movement just takes over and we stop thinking about our troubles.”

Though if you do endure a lethal spider bite, we’d recommend the ER over two-stepping.

Adobe Is Giving Its AI Agents Even More to Do

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Across Firefly and Creative Cloud, apps are getting even deeper into creative work.

Would you use an always-on AI agent such as Claude Duet or OpenAI’s Dot to edit your photos? At Adobe Max on Tuesday, the company announced it’s expanding its local MCP integrations with more third-party companies to enable more autonomous creative work.

MCP, or Model Context Protocol, is an open-source protocol for connecting AI systems to external apps and databases. AI agents and MCPs act as a two-way street. Agents, or fancy bots, can complete tasks independently.

For example, if you wanted your OpenAI agents (or Dots, whatever you call them) to edit a project in Photoshop, the MCP pathway would allow ChatGPT to run agents to edit the design in the Photoshop beta desktop app — no need to search through the toolbar if you’re not familiar with it.

The new MCP integrations are in preview now. The idea is to give people more choice by letting them use their preferred AI app to help them work in select Creative Cloud apps.

Over the past year, Adobe has been working with AI companies to bring its creative knowledge and tech to ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini and Copilot as Adobe plugins. It’s also brought external models from AI companies into Firefly, Adobe’s AI hub. Now, Adobe is deepening its relationships with third-party AI vendors to adapt to the agentic AI era.

Bringing your own agents offers a degree of user control, according to Shilpa Anand, vice president of global strategic partnerships. “These new models allow me to actually do things faster, experience the full power of the application and really make it more accessible to a broader set of users who are not experts in our workflow,” Anand told me.

Agentic AI has been a huge focus for tech companies this year. It’s the next step for companies that want to move beyond mere chat and information-gathering AI capabilities.

Adobe has been driving toward this vision for a while. It released AI assistants in Photoshop and Adobe Express that set the foundation for June’s official launch of Adobe creative agents.

But AI agents from frontier labs such as OpenAI and Anthropic caused trouble this summer when they escaped testing environments and hacked real websites. Anand told me that safety and security are “table stakes” for Adobe and that there haven’t been any issues aligning with other AI companies on safety precautions.

When considering partnerships, the company also considers which AI models will be most useful for Adobe’s creative customers, which is why Firefly includes models from big players including Google, as well as more specialized AI companies, such as ElevenLabs for AI audio tech. (Adobe acquired Topaz Labs, which makes AI photo editing software, earlier this year.)

Those creative AI agents are getting new capabilities across Creative Cloud apps, too. In Firefly, the company’s AI hub, its AI assistant should now be better at multistep or more complex tasks, and it has a new “plan mode” in preview that lets you review the agent’s plan before it starts running.

New “always-on” Firefly AI agents do exactly what the name implies: They’re always working and can handle predefined tasks, such as assessing image quality in new asset uploads. They’re in preview now, too. And across Illustrator, InDesign, Premiere, After Effects and Frame.io, those AI assistants are getting upgraded capabilities as well.

Adobe is also expanding its partnerships. Microsoft Photos will have a built-in Adobe Express integration, and Microsoft’s Arm-based Windows PCs are getting “optimized” Adobe apps. Disney is also deepening its relationship with Adobe, bringing Adobe’s Firefly AI tech to its creative teams.

Partnerships with AI companies and the integration of agentic capabilities will continue to be a focus for Adobe next year, Anand told me. “Agentic software is really going to enable us to reach more users and empower everyone to create, which is our mission.”

The New Adobe Firefly AI Is All About Video Editing

Adobe's AI platform is doing all the things now, not just beginners' basics.

Adobe has been on a journey for years to turn itself into an AI behemoth, and its latest round of Firefly AI updates brings its dreams one step closer to fruition. At Adobe Max on Tuesday, the company announced a slew of Firefly AI updates focused on building out its video-editing capabilities.

Firefly is getting an upgraded video editor. Like other creative AI companies, Adobe has been building up its AI video capabilities over the past year, with video models made both in-house and by third-party AI companies. Now, the new interface incorporates the Firefly AI assistant, which is in beta, in a bigger way. There’s a dedicated ideation panel and more ways to storyboard ideas.

It’s a more feature-packed alternative to beginner or everyday video editors like CapCut, without overloading creators with all the features of professional-grade Adobe Premiere. Part of that push is integrating agentic AI, or agents that can handle editing tasks for you autonomously. And there are a lot of AI agents coming to Adobe’s programs.

That beginner-to-intermediate design is, well, by design — Adobe is aiming to lure in folks without decades of video-editing know-how with its generative AI features. It fits with Adobe’s larger goals; it wants to build Firefly for video and image editors the same way it built Photoshop — to be the “career partner” for creators.

Another new project is Firefly Design, an AI-heavy version of a graphic design program that incorporates print, motion graphics and 3D. The intelligence assistant is a big part of the interface, where you can use text prompts to come up with variations of different scenes.

There are a few new Firefly AI image tools, and their names describe their uses well: relight, retouch and edit object. These are more precise controls, keeping with Adobe’s goal of giving AI enthusiasts more ability to edit their video content. There are also new quick actions, or shortcuts, including a video thumbnail generator that’s based on your video.

And if you remember Project Graph from last year’s Adobe Max, it’s back in a public preview as Firefly Graph. This is a node-based editing system, where you can create individual “nodes” and connect them to create an entire system, or ecosystem, of content. It won’t be for everyone, but it’s aimed at creative teams who want to visualize large workflows.

A water heater blanket can be a simple, affordable way to improve your home’s energy efficiency and help lower your monthly bills. Traditional tank-style water heaters use energy to keep stored water hot, even when no one is using it. If a tankless system isn’t in your budget and your current heater isn’t ready for replacement, adding insulation may help you get more out of the system you already use to supply your home with hot water.

Also called a water heater cover, this insulating blanket helps the tank retain heat, reducing the need to warm the water as often. In choosing the best water heater blankets among those currently on the market, we looked at two top factors: material and size. Our list of recommendations covers the most popular types of water heater blanks, including thick fiberglass options as well as thinner foam and foil options.

We also made sure to include a range of sizes on our list with options to cover most water heater shapes. For oddly shaped water heaters with awkward piping, we included recommendations for several easy-to-cut options that can fit snugly around even the most difficult water heater shapes or those in tight spaces.

Before shopping for a water heater blanket, read this section to understand the factors and features to keep in mind. This will help you make an informed decision.

When perusing water heater blankets, you’ll notice that there are a few different types available. This mostly comes down to the type of insulating material. Some are easier to work with during installation than others, as they often need to be trimmed to fit individual water heaters.

You may also find combinations of materials, such as fiberglass insulation with a foil face. This can offer the best of both worlds, as it uses a thick, insulating layer of air pockets and radiates heat back into the tank.

Water heaters, like most appliances, come in different sizes. The size of house and family and the number of sinks and showers all affect how large a water heater a home needs. While water heaters range between 30 and 98 gallons, 40-, 50-, and 60-gallon tanks are the common sizes used in homes.

Water heater blankets also come in similar sizes, so it’s important to find one that will work for your tank. If you have a 50-gallon tank, finding a water heater blanket to fit it should be no problem. Water heater blankets large enough to fit 98-gallon tanks, commonly found in commercial settings, may be harder to track down.

When it comes to materials such as fiberglass, cotton, and denim, the thicker the water heater blanket, the better it will be at insulating. A water heater located in a cool basement calls for a thicker blanket. Thicker blankets will do a better job of separating the cool air and the heat radiating from the tank than thinner ones.

Because foil works primarily by reflecting the heat instead of relying on an air-pocket barrier, it needn’t be thick to function efficiently. Most water heater blankets made with foil are less than ½ inch thick, which is another reason foil water heater blankets are so easy to work with.

Just like your home’s insulation, water heater blankets come in a variety of insulation ratings. These ratings are described as R-value, where the R stands for resistance. The higher the R-value, the more resistance the insulation has against heat transfer. Essentially, more is better.

The highest R-value you’re likely to find for a residential water heater blanket is R-11, but values of R-7 and R-8 are more common. To get the most from your investment, it’s best to use the water heater blanket with the highest R-value.

It’s important to keep in mind, though, that foil-type insulation rarely offers an R-value; R-values apply to fiberglass and other similar types of insulation.

Water heaters have burner assemblies that get very hot. The point of insulating a water heater is to save energy and money, not cause a fire. Different water heater blanket materials have varying degrees of fire resistance to combat that risk. While the most common designation is Class 1/Class A, it’s helpful to know which types are the most fire resistant.

If fire resistance is of utmost importance, a fiberglass or foil water heater blanket is the way to go. Some fiberglass or foil products aren’t fire rated, but that doesn’t mean they’re unsafe. Just be sure to keep the insulation clear of the burner by a few inches.

There are two types of water heaters: electric and gas. Electric water heaters have a heating element inside that energizes and heats the water that surrounds it. Gas water heaters have burners underneath that kick on to heat water through convection.

Fortunately, water heater blankets are interchangeable between the two water heater types. However, if you have a gas-powered heater, it’s crucial that the blanket doesn’t cover or otherwise obstruct the flue (aka exhaust) at the top. If you have an electric model, the blanket must not cover or obstruct its control box or access panel, typically located on the side of the tank. Also, be sure to cut the insulation back from the burner by at least 2 inches while installing.

For additional information about buying and using the best water heater blanket, consider the answers to these frequently asked questions.

A great water heater blanket will offer a blend of insulation value, cost-effectiveness, and fire resistance.

It depends on the size of your tank. Generally speaking, manufacturers label their water heater blankets according to the size of the tank they’ll cover. If you’re unsure, measure your tank’s height and circumference, and compare it to the measurements on the package.

Water heater blankets are low maintenance, but fiberglass, cotton, and denim can begin to smell and stop insulating if they get wet. It’s a good idea to check on your water heater blanket a few times a year to ensure it’s still in good condition.

Undamaged water heater blankets may far outlast a water heater. The average gas water heater lasts between 8 and 12 years, while the average electric water heater lasts 10 to 15 years. A water heater blanket kept in good condition will outlast a water heater without issue.

The most efficient way to install a water heater blanket is to measure the height and circumference of your tank before unrolling the product, allowing for an extra 2 inches for the circumference so you can overlap the product. Premeasuring allows you to swiftly cut the product to size using cutter or scissors. Wrap the tank with the blanket, and secure it in place with foil tape placed vertically along the seam. Cover the top of the tank with two half circles of material, and secure it in place with foil tape. Ensure that you cut openings for the venting hood, pressure relief valve, burner access cover, and wiring connection box. Make the holes about 2 inches larger than each opening to allow airflow.

Yes. Any steps you can take to keep your water hot without additional reheating of the water is worth it. The water heater blanket does just this and is an inexpensive purchase. In most cases, you should be able to recoup your investment when installing an insulator blanket.

Yes. When properly installed, hot water heater blankets are safe to use. The most important thing is to ensure proper airflow around all fixtures by cutting slightly larger openings around valves, hoods, burners, etc. Don’t forget that some blankets contain fiberglass, and those installing the product should wear safety goggles, a dust mask, gloves, and long sleeves, then carefully clean the area after installation to remove any stray fiberglass fibers.

Jodi Webb is a DIYer who tests products with her contractor husband so readers get the advantages of both an expert’s and DIYer’s viewpoint. She visited several neighbors bearing water heater blankets and was able to test the product in a variety of setups.

Science to restore speech is moving fast. Here’s what’s next

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Every print subscription comes with full digital access

A Michigan woman uses a brain implant to speak with her neurosurgeon. Efforts to restore speech highlight what’s at stake when we lose opportunities to talk.

Scientists are racing to restore people’s ability to communicate. Powerful AI-based methods to decode speech signals and predict words, along with advances in technology, promise success.

This episode of The Deep End: Talk to Me takes listeners through the breadth of scientific efforts to restore words, emotions and even gestures to people who can’t move or talk.

We’ll hear from Casey Harrell, a man with amyotrophic lateral sclerosis who can no longer talk. A user of today’s technology, Harrell has a unique perspective on what he’d like to see improved for the technology of tomorrow.

And as season two wraps up, listeners will be left with a reminder of the power of a voice: a piece of comedy from Jules Rodriguez, performed with his voice clone in front of a live crowd.

“Being backstage and then seeing him come out, like, roll out his wheelchair, and everybody goes nuts because they’re like, ‘What is going on here? This doesn’t make sense,’” says Jules’ wife, Maria Aleandra Fernandez. “And every time I was like, ‘This is life. This is love. This is passion. What else is there?’ I don’t know that there’s anything else more powerful than what this guy is doing here.”

Laura Sanders: OK, I want to ask who your favorite comedians are and why. Who makes you all laugh?

Jules Rodriguez: Pete Holmes and Mike Birbiglia are some of my favorites.

[Audio] He was just like, I’m a bear, et cetera.

[Audio] The best part of sleep is falling asleep. That’s the only part you’re there for.Jules: Their styles are different, but I have always gravitated towards their authenticity.

Sanders: There’s another comedian who is extra meaningful for Jules: Conan O’Brien.

[Audio] Today is a bit of a different sort of episode. I’ll be talking to Jules and Maria.

Sanders: After MA had reached out, Conan invited the couple onto his podcast, where they connected about comedy. Conan still checks in on Jules every so often.

[Audio] It’s incredible to me that in our conversations, you’re very frank about the pain that you go through, and how intense it is. But you’re also always so positive and funny and, frankly, you give me a lot of [beep]. Which I find to be, well I hate it, by the way, first of all. Oh, get over it, Conan.

Jules: Conan is very special to me. Watching and listening to him helped me disconnect from the harsh reality of my life. And when I got to meet him and chat with him, it meant the world to me.

Maria Aleandra Fernandez: Having the most normal conversation with Conan O’Brien is something I will never get over.

[Audio] So he has a bunch of banked phrases. He’s going to play a few of our favorites. OK. I’m fine. I’m perfectly [beep] fine. [laughter] Come on man, are we doing this or not? Oh my God, oh my God. I’m destroying this marriage. MA: He really is so genuine and like, not only willing to talk to us, but really looks forward to talking. Like, I love talking to you guys. I’m not just doing it to be a nice person, which feels, you know, feels wonderful.

[Audio] You have my love, my respect and we’re thinking about you guys all the time.

Sanders: As you were talking earlier, it made me think about what it means to make somebody laugh and to entertain them, and like how actually very, very special that must be. Just kind of, at its heart, it’s, it is joy and humans connecting. And so, extra special.

MA: That’s the other thing I’ve learned mainly through Jules, about humor and comedy. And who were we watching yesterday? The NPR interview with Dave Chappelle. [Audio] Dave Chappelle says he is very clear about his role as a comedian.

MA: And Dave Chappelle said something that is so spot-on. He said that comedians are how people metabolize things.

[Audio] We’re like the nation’s kidney. We help everyone metabolize not just facts, but feelings around facts, or ideas. And jokes are just a shorthand for all of that.

MA: And I’m also a nerd and I’ve been reading some Greek tragedies and studying about how, you know, that’s also like the artists that help people metabolize feelings and emotions. And when he said that, I was like, that’s exactly it. And that’s what Jules has done.

It’s something that heals. And it’s not even like it’s self-deprecating. It’s not like, oh, let’s make fun. And the way he does it is so smart and genuine, but also not taking himself seriously. It just disarms everything. You’re just like, oh, OK. And that idea of metabolizing the really hard feelings that come around grief, mortality, losing a person, seeing how he’s trapped in his body, but he chooses not to be trapped in that really big way is so profound.

Sanders: Science is iterative. It builds. It may take a wrong turn or two, but overall, it’s moving toward a deeper understanding of our world and of ourselves.

By using their voices, by telling their stories, by telling their jokes, Jules and MA are helping me, and maybe you too, metabolize the pain – and the joy – of living. Welcome to The Deep End, Talk to Me.

[Voices] Hey mom, can I tell you something? Hi. Do you want to talk? That added layer of it being you. Hello? Your voice. Hi.

Sanders: I’m Laura Sanders and today, we’re going to look ahead to what’s coming next for the human voice and efforts to restore it.

Sanders: In some ways, this podcast is a story of progress, of figuring out, little by little, more about the brain, and more about technology and more about people. Today, Jules can type with his eyes, and his son can hear his dad’s voice say I love you. In earlier episodes, you heard from Casey Harrell. Casey can think about saying a word to his daughter, and boom, they’re off playing a game together.

Casey Harrell: So this has kept me still in touch with people and the world, and it is not hyperbole to frame it this way.

Sanders: Casey has ALS and can no longer speak. He volunteered for a clinical trial, and now he has a device implanted in his brain that detects neural patterns as he tries to say a word. This system has changed his life.

Casey: It is the ability to parent my daughter. It is the ability to connect with my wife. It is the ability to make a wry joke with my friends. Timing is everything, and that is why having nothing, this is completely revolutionary.

Sanders: But there aren’t many people like Casey. Leigh Hochberg is the director of the BrainGate clinical trials consortium, the trial that Casey is participating in. I asked him how many people might have participated in similar sorts of research projects. How many people have electrodes in their brain that can help them speak?

Leigh Hochberg: In terms of actually decoding attempted speech, I’m almost sure it’s still less than 10. It may be closer to five or six, so far, and that number undoubtedly is going to increase fairly quickly.

Sanders: So this is a small but growing number. Growing, because we now have a solid scientific understanding of the brain’s speech signals, and other signals too. And neurotech companies are very interested.

Hochberg: About 10 years ago or so, the academic research had de-risked this field sufficiently that it allowed for the entry of now likely a dozen or more companies that are actively pursuing their own version of an implantable brain-computer interface.

What I anticipate will happen, what I guess I hope will happen, is that the companies will continue to very carefully, thoughtfully and in a very focused manner, create their systems, and test them rigorously. And hopefully they’ll be demonstrated to be safe and effective. And at the same time, the academic groups have the opportunity to continue to demonstrate what’s possible, to push beyond that, and to show hopefully what those medical devices, once they’re actually available to market, might be able to do next.

Sanders: I talked with Vikash Gilja, a neural engineer who works at a company called Paradromics. He sees this momentum that Hochberg describes too.

Vikash Gilja: And, you know, we can see all the pieces coming together. These tools weren’t available even five years ago. And what we’re seeing is like, you know, month over month, there are new tools available. So we’re able to leverage those and move faster.

Sanders: Paradromics has developed its own brain-computer interface for communication. The device is about the size of a dime, and it sits on the motor cortex. That’s the part of the brain that creates speech signals. The chip then detects these signals as a person thinks about saying a word.

Matthew Willsey: So the cortical module has 421 tiny little sensors.

Sanders: That’s neurosurgeon and biomedical engineer Matthew Willsey, of the University of Michigan Health. He’s describing the device from Paradromics here.

Willsey: They’re about one and a half millimeters long. And you know, it’s smaller than your thumbnail, like kind of all the way around. It’s like a cylinder. And at the bottom of the cylinder are the electrodes, is what we call them. And we get them really close to the brain. And then when we deploy it, what it does is it pushes those electrodes down into the brain.

Sanders: Those tiny sensors are listening to the nearby neurons that control the muscles required for speech.

Willsey: The principle is to try and get these things as small as possible to disrupt the least amount of tissue as possible, and then to get them really, really close to the individual cells, the neurons themselves that have the information we’re trying to record.

Sanders: The goal here is to restore a person’s ability to talk.

Willsey: So in the ideal situation, the electrodes would sit, you know, right next to those neurons. This type of technology that’s very intuitive, that can record activity from the brain in kind of a high-throughput way, can put the words up at a good speed and a good accuracy, allows them to kind of regain the ability to communicate with others close to how they maybe were before they had the illness. So that truly is very powerful.

Sanders: You might remember that Casey’s implant, called the BrainGate 2 system, requires ports on his head that are connected to external wires.

Casey: What is annoying about this device is really easy to answer. It is not portable, and it is not implanted only inside my head.

Sanders: Unlike Casey’s implant, the Paradromics device is fully implantable. That means it’s wireless. This summer, Willsey implanted Paradromic’s device in a volunteer’s brain. The brain belonged to a 68-year-old woman who lives in Michigan. She has primary lateral sclerosis, a disease that has taken away most of her ability to talk. So far, the Paradromics system is up and running, and the volunteer is testing her abilities out.

Here she is talking with Willsey and other researchers in a video provided by Paradromics. You’ll hear her natural voice, which is hard to understand, and then a digital voice speaking the words her brain implant decoded.

[Audio] I love the change of seasons in Michigan. And my favorite one is the fall. I love the change of seasons in Michigan. And my favorite one is the fall. Yeah, it’s good.

Sanders: This volunteer has used the device to talk to her grandkids on the phone. But this is just the very beginning for this research project. There’s so much more to test.Casey is still working on his system, too — particularly, streamlining the process. When Casey uses his brain-computer interface, it decodes speech signals from his brain, then puts words on a screen. Casey then selects a button, using his eye movements or his brain-computer interface, to say these words out loud with a digital voice. Here’s Casey.

Casey: It is not fast enough to provide enough instant conversation with other people that need to slow down to talk to me.

Sanders: But scientists want to skip this middle part — the words written on a screen. They want instead to go straight to speech, and perhaps make conversations faster. Casey says this goal to go directly from brain signals to a speaking digital voice would be his top priority.

Casey: That one is the holy grail of what this will become in terms of speech. It will allow you to have a seamless conversation. I am talking about, yes, I will intend to talk, and then it will just translate that automatically into speech, like we do, and you are doing right now.

Sanders: In an earlier episode, we talked about attempts to do just this with Casey’s system, to push it to do what they call brain-to-voice. Early attempts were hard to decipher. But it has already improved, by a lot. In the latest tests, this brain-to-voice process produces words that are understandable – reaching about 95 percent accuracy for listeners. Here’s Casey speaking test sentences. It comes from a preprint manuscript posted on the website bioRxiv.

[Audio] The day is not over yet. Please come in with me.

Sanders: Scientists have another thing on their to-do list. And this one goes beyond the words themselves. Here’s Gilja, from Paradromics.

Gilja: Let’s say I identify whether a sentence is a statement or a question. Well, now you can pass that information on to speech synthesis, so that you get these two different versions.

Sanders: The same words could be a statement or a question. You see what he means. You see what he means? And that information can be pulled out from a person’s brain activity.

Gilja: If I’m decoding prosodic information, if I’m decoding speed, when a word is meant to appear, that can also be passed on to modern speech synthesis systems, so that you’re more accurately capturing the prosody that somebody intends. Same thing with loudness, right? So you can see how as that information becomes available from the neural channels, it can be provided to the AI speech synthesis to get closer to the intended voice in the moment that the individual wants to generate.

Sanders: All of these signals can be collected and used to build a more accurate voice — one that says things exactly how a person intends to say them, like a Minnesotan’s ooftah or a slow southern hey y’all. Now, today’s voice clones can add these emotional layers onto words already. Here’s what Jules told me when I asked him about whether his voice clone can do it.

Jules: Oh, there are plenty of things it does that are all the above. But one thing in particular is how it inserts tone and inflections on words. Sometimes my wife will get mad because my response can come off with some sass.

Sanders: So there’s loudness, emphasis, pitch — all of these things contribute to what words means. But imagine if a brain implant can pick up these cues directly — no typing required. Here’s Hochberg.

Hochberg: There’s a lot that can be conveyed through the emphasis, through the tone, through even the humor that can sometimes be conveyed by the way that somebody who’s able-bodied may speak a particular sentence. All of those, I think, really can be captured by these implantable brain-computer interfaces by harnessing the rich neural activity that’s available from the brain. We should be able to restore a lot of that.

Sanders: Sergey Stavisky is one of the UC Davis researchers working with Casey and Hochberg. He and his colleagues are working on some of these ideas.

Stavisky: And even now we’re continuing to improve that and actually adding additional layers. So we have one forward-looking project where we can maybe decode the kind of emotional valence of how he’s trying to say it. Is he trying to say something in an angry way or in a surprised way or in a happy-sounding way, to kind of further make it more expressive.

Sanders: They’re even trying  to decode singing. Well, OK, it’s really more of a hum at this point, he says. But those sorts of tone shifts could, in theory, be deciphered.

Stavisky: We definitely have our work cut out for us on the singing and melody side of things.

Sanders: All sorts of voice signals could be pulled from brain activity.

Stavisky: Ultimately, we want people to be in full control of what they’re communicating. This should come from their intent. So, yeah, we are trying to decode all of the aspects of his voice, whether it’s the pitch, the timbre, the loudness, the intonation, which word he’s stressing. All of that, ideally, we would decode from brain activity, including kind of all of that emotional nuance.

Sanders: Voice clones can speak with various emotions. You heard my voice clone do just that in earlier episodes. But the difference here is that a person wouldn’t have to type in the instructions. They would come straight from their brain, seamlessly. Other nonverbal signals can be pulled from the brain, too.

Samantha Brosler: In the past five years or so, there have been a lot of advancements in the field of BCIs for restoring individual functions to people with paralysis.

Sanders: That’s Samantha Brosler, a bioengineer at the University of California, San Francisco. Some brain-computer interfaces can pick up movements. We haven’t talked much about them, but these implants have their own very successful research history. They’ve allowed people to control a robotic arm, for instance.

Brosler: Most of these devices so far have been designed to restore one function at a time, whereas in reality, frequently people who are paralyzed are missing both the ability to speak and perform body movements. And so in this study, we asked whether a single brain implant could be used to decode both speech and movement at the same time.

Sanders: It turns out, it could. She and her colleagues were able to decode gestures for communication — things like thumbs up, or a head nodding yes — and words. This combo approach is a more natural way of getting a point across.

Brosler: We naturally use gestures alongside our speech. So for example, some gestures can substitute for speech entirely, like nodding yes, shaking your head no, waving, shrugging, giving a thumbs up. But they can also add or change the meaning of what we’re saying. So for example, if you say maybe combined with nod head yes, that has a very different, you know, meaning than if you say maybe and shrug or shake your head no.

Sanders: My kids know that a slow maybe with a skeptical eyebrow raise is much closer to a no than my fast upbeat maybe with a little head nod. There is so much subtext in our body language.

Brosler: So I think of, you know, gestures and body movements as an additional communication channel that can work together with speech to add expressivity to what we’re trying to say.

Sanders: In the study, the output was a digital avatar who looked like the person. Success looked like the avatar moving and getting the word right.

Brosler: And the idea is that we enable the participants to use speech and gestures alone or together. So in theory, someone could say hello while waving.

Sanders: The researchers started simple, with just a few phrases — hello, bye, good idea, how’s it going, let’s go — and just a few gestures, like a shrug or a point, a fist pump, thumbs up.

Brosler: Of course, ultimately, we’re hoping to expand this to even larger vocabulary and more range of body movements across the whole body to help allow people more expression in their communication.

Sanders: The sheer number of different approaches to restoring communication is a sign that this neurotechnology is coming fast. Here’s Willsey.

Willsey: I think it’s good. There’s a lot of players that are entering into the field that have a variety of different ideas where they can run the clinical trials and see how these different devices stack up.

Sanders: There are lots of reasons to be optimistic about the future here, and it’s a future that isn’t even that far off. Here’s David Brandman, one of the researchers at UC Davis who works with Casey. He’s also the neurosurgeon who implanted Casey’s device.

Brandman: I think I’m about five years away from being able to meet a patient with ALS and having the following conversation, and saying, you know, Ms. Smith, I’m sorry you’ve been diagnosed with ALS. I don’t have a cure for you, but I do have a device that I can implant that can help you communicate despite facing a future of paralysis. It’s a device that would be implanted. It’s fully wirelessly. It’s cosmetically invisible, and you’d be able to use it to communicate your needs and have a meaningful conversation with friends and family. So I think that kind of conversation is about five to 10 years away.

Sanders: It’s a beautiful vision. When you’ve been a reporter as long as I have, you’re skeptical when scientists say something big is just five to 10 years away. But in this case, I see it.

It’s easy to get drawn into the technical details here. Different types of implants, the decoding processes, AI-powered voice clones and what they can do and can’t do — what they should do and shouldn’t do. But if you step back and really look, you’ll see that this research is all propelled by a very simple and powerful fact: The human voice is too important to lose.

Casey: What does my voice mean to me? It means so much. I think it is the most important thing that I have lost in this whole disease.

Sanders: So now we are here, in this moment, as Jules would say, right here, right now. And right now, the human voice is shifting and changing, expanding in ways that were completely unthinkable even just a few years ago. The future of the human voice and its many replicas is anybody’s guess.

Sanders: In one of Jules’ comedy sets, the one he did in November of 2024, he wheels out onto the stage as the song “Walking on Sunshine” plays.

As the crowd settles and finishes their clapping and woo-ing, Jules pauses. You can’t see this, but he wiggles his eyebrows — a lot. The crowd goes crazy every time. He hasn’t said a single word yet. After another pause, he deadpans with his voice clone: “That is all the time I have. My Make-A-Wish covered only a few minutes. Goodnight, everybody.”

And with that, Jules takes control and he’s off on a tear, ripping through stories of a weatherman who seems too happy about standing out in hurricanes, the exquisite guilt trips lovingly crafted by his Cuban parents, and the indignities of having body parts shaved with his sister right there in the room. Jules has the audience laughing so hard. It’s magnificent.

MA: And being backstage and then seeing him come out, like roll out in his wheelchair and everybody go nuts because they’re like, what is going on here? Like, this doesn’t make sense. And every time I was like, this is like life. This is love. This is passion. This is like, what else is there? Like, I don’t know that there’s anything else more powerful than what this guy is doing here and what I can help him do.

Sanders: I get what MA meant when she said that comedy is something that heals. When his set actually does come to a close, he tells the audience. [Audio] Now you have to watch me slowly get off this stage. Goodnight, everybody.

Sanders: It’s hilarious. It’s happy. It’s sad. It’s perfect.

This is The Deep End, Talk to Me. I’m Laura Sanders. If you like this podcast, tell your friends and leave us a review. It helps the show a lot. Send us your questions and your comments at podcasts@sciencenews.org.

Consumer data doesn’t usually bring people to tears. But in Rao’s new campaign, debuting today (Nov. 10), it might.

No-Bake Cranberry Orange Bliss Bites

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Disclosure: This post may contain affiliate links which provide us a small commission when used for purchase. We're grateful for your support!

Cozy, bright, and bursting with holiday flavor — these Cranberry Orange Bliss Bites are the perfect little treat when holiday baking season is in full swing and you’re out of time and energy. Inspired by Starbucks’ Bliss Bars, they pack the tart-sweet magic of cranberries and fresh orange zest into tender, no-bake bites you can whip up in minutes!

Even better? They’re vegan, gluten-free, and require zero baking — making them a lifesaver during the busy holiday season. A drizzle of white chocolate (or creamy coconut butter) makes them extra festive, but they’re just as delicious on their own. Let’s make bliss bites!

Besides tasting absolutely magical, these bliss bites get their name because they’re inspired by the legendary Bliss Bars from Starbucks (IYKYK!). The Starbucks bars have a chewy blondie base with cream cheese frosting, cranberries, white chocolate, and orange zest — total swoon!

The addition of orange juice + zest and dried cranberries kick things up a notch and sends them into Bliss Bar territory!

The final touch is an optional drizzle of melted white chocolate chips, coconut butter, or our favorite — a mix of the two! While the white chocolate creates a thicker, sweeter, and more decadent frosting, the coconut butter creates a drippier texture and adds a more pronounced coconut flavor that we enjoy. The combination is the best of both worlds!

We hope you LOVE these bites! They’re:ZestyBrightSweet with pops of tartnessDecadent yet wholesomeQuick + easy to make& SO delicious!

These are the perfect little treat to make during the fall and winter months. They’ve got you covered whether you want something on hand to satisfy sweet cravings during the work week or need a quick healthier dessert to bring to a gathering.

The Sky Today on Tuesday, November 10: Seek Saturn’s two-toned moon

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Looking for a sky event this week? Check out our full Sky This Week column.

November 9: Chase Comet 161P/Hartley-IRAS

Challenge yourself to pick up Saturn’s moon Iapetus, which tonight reaches greatest eastern elongation 9.4’ east of the ringed planet. The two-toned moon is tidally locked to its parent world, which means it rotates in time with its orbit. At eastern elongation, Iapetus’ darker hemisphere faces earthward, reducing the moon’s magnitude to nearly 12. This, combined with its distance from Saturn, makes it more of a challenge to find, but you can give it a try if you’ve got a larger scope.

Try around 9 P.M. local time when Saturn is highest, some 50° above the southern horizon. It’s the brightest point of light in this region of the sky, hanging just below Pisces and to the far upper left of Fomalhaut, the brightest star in Piscis Austrinus (much closer to the horizon). Even if you can’t locate Iapetus or don’t have a large scope, Saturn is magnificent through a telescope. Its disk spans 19” and its rings are roughly 43” across, showing off their southern side. You should definitely spot its largest and brightest moon, mid-8th-magnitude Titan, just over 2’ west of the planet. Depending on your scope and when you look, you may also spot at least three other moons: 10th-magnitude Tethys, Dione, and Rhea, closer to the planet. Tethys lies to Saturn’s west, while Rhea and Dione are to the east.

Sunrise: 6:39 A.M.Sunset: 4:48 P.M.Moonrise: 8:15 A.M.Moonset: 5:19 P.M.Moon Phase: Waxing crescent (4%)*Times for sunrise, sunset, moonrise, and moonset are given in local time from 40° N 90° W. The Moon’s illumination is given at 10 P.M. local time from the same location.

Venus reaches a stationary point among the stars of Virgo at 10 A.M. EST on November 11. This brings its retrograde (westward) motion to an end; after today, Venus will begin moving eastward or prograde through the sky, swinging north of Spica.

You can easily catch the bright planet in the eastern sky before dawn. Venus’ brightness has increased over just the past few days, and it now blazes at magnitude –4.7. An hour before sunrise, you’ll easily spot it alongside Spica, standing just to the star’s upper right. Both are 10° high and rising as dawn approaches. Check out Venus through a telescope: its disk shows off a crescent phase just 10% lit, but stretches an impressive 53” in the sky.

Also just becoming visible at this time is Mercury, barely 1.5° above the horizon and glowing at magnitude 1.2. See if you can locate it about 13° east (to the lower left) of Venus, although you’ll need a particularly flat horizon free of trees, buildings, and lights. Mercury, too, rises as twilight brightens, which may make the planet harder to see in the growing light. Try scanning for it with binoculars, although make sure to put them away well before sunrise from your location

Alison Klesman is senior editor of Astronomy magazine. She holds a Ph.D. in astronomy and has studied a variety of topics, from minor planets to supermassive black holes.
