Aberdeen hero's sobering verdict on lost identity, Cormack's big headache and Pittodrie's revolving door

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Alex McLeish reckons Aberdeen chairman Dave Cormack will be “agonising” over their current predicament, suggesting he faces a huge challenge to restore the club's standing as a powerhouse of Scottish football amid another deeply disappointing campaign.

The Dons' ongoing struggles resulted in manager Stephen Robinson being sacked on Monday, with the Northern Irishman relieved of his duties after eight months in charge as results continued to fall short of expectations.

Did you know with a Digital Subscription to The Scotsman, you can get unlimited access to the website including our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

Aberdeen’s hierarchy scheduled a hastily-arranged meeting to discuss the 51-year-old’s future in the wake of a 4-0 defeat at home to Hibs on Saturday, which left the Pittodrie side languishing in 11th place in the Scottish Premiership table.

With concerns surrounding the direction of the club, McLeish feels the problems confronting Aberdeen extend beyond the man in the dugout as he pointed towards poor recruitment, player confidence and a lack of collective identity as key issues which have all resulted in Robinson’s downfall.

Having witnessed the commitment Cormack has shown to the club, McLeish believes he will be feeling the weight of responsibility more than most after standing by his beloved team through some particularly difficult periods.

“Dave is a close friend and I know he's done everything in his power to try and get the Dons to a level,” he said. “We've seen a Scottish Cup final victory a couple of seasons ago. But the league positions are not what Aberdeen fans expect.

‘He will be agonising. You know what it’s like if you get something wrong, you can't sleep at night, can you? Dave will have been through all that kind of rigmarole as well. There'll be a big burden on him right now.

“But he's been stoic and he's stood by the club. He has to be given massive credit for that. It's just about getting Aberdeen to cement their name back at the higher echelons of Scottish football.”

That 2025 Scottish Cup triumph under Jimmy Thelin had offered hope that Aberdeen could use it as a springboard towards challenging more consistently at the top end of the league. Instead, the club have found themselves repeatedly struggling to establish the momentum required to compete with the likes of Celtic, Rangers and Hearts.

The frustration for McLeish is that the rebuilding process has yet to deliver the sustained progress supporters have been demanding.

“I've not got a solution unfortunately, but it's obviously down to acquiring players of a certain level,” he declared. “So, do they have to look at their recruitment? Is it just players that have lost their identity, lost their confidence? These are two massive factors in the world of football.”

The inevitable consequence of a prolonged run of poor results is that the manager becomes the most vulnerable figure. Robinson found himself in precisely that situation, with McLeish claiming that the pressure may have become impossible to withstand as results showed no signs of improving.

He has sympathy for Robinson, particularly given the difficulties of trying to turn around a struggling team when the responsibility for recruitment and squad construction is shared across the club.

“Is it down to recruitment? Are the players not good enough? The manager will always get the worse side of it, because they can't sack 20 players. It's sad to see. I like Stevie, I met him a few times down south and I thought he'd be good for Aberdeen. But, like everybody else, it's trying to get a team to function together.

”To have forwards that you know can score, to have midfielders that can show a bit of quality, eating a man, one-twos, being accurate with your passing. Every bit of quality factor comes into it. I'm just hoping Stevie can get a couple of good results, but it may even be too late, I don't know.”

The wider concern is Aberdeen's inability to re-establish themselves as a club opponents genuinely fear. McLeish believes fans have been waiting several years for a sustained return to prominence, with occasional moments of encouragement failing to translate into lasting progress.

“We've been waiting on it for four or five years. There's been some signs,” he reflected. “There's been obviously winning the cup that year. You think that's a wee springboard for the coming season and then it's all kind of middle of the league, lower end of the league. What is the solution? It's definitely always down to what kind of players you've got.”

*Alex McLeish was speaking at the announcement of William Hill as Official Sponsor and Proper Supporter of Walking Football Scotland. To find your nearest game visit walkingfootballscotland.org.

Art, Architecture, Archaeology: A Refuge for Artists at Amman’s Darat al Funun

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

At a time when public support for the arts is under pressure in many countries, Darat al Funun — literally "House of the Arts" — stands by its long-standing mission to champion artists. "Artists are witnesses of their time," affirms Suha Shoman, its founder. "Maybe Art does not operate change, but it does record it […] it points fingers. At the same time, it speaks of, even if it's so difficult and at times so violent, it speaks of beauty as well."

At a time when public support for the arts is under pressure in many countries, Darat al Funun — literally "House of the Arts" — stands by its long-standing mission to champion artists. "Artists are witnesses of their time," affirms Suha Shoman, its founder. "Maybe Art does not operate change, but it does record it […] it points fingers. At the same time, it speaks of, even if it's so difficult and at times so violent, it speaks of beauty as well."

A place where the past remains present, Darat al Funun spreads in the hills of Jabal al Luweibdeh, on a rugged terrain overlooking the eye of downtown Amman, Jordan. Its landscape is warmly inviting and accessible to the public, with a lower entrance in the south and an upper one in the north. Across its cascading terraces, the site is visually permeable as well, demonstrating the foundation's core tenet: art is a civic necessity and a vessel for history.

Can you still trust Proton?

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

The Swiss software company has built its reputation on a commitment to privacy. Did its CEO just destroy the trust of its customer base?

It hasn’t been a good month for Proton, the Swiss technology company best known for privacy-focused products like Proton Mail and Proton VPN. Now, in a blog post published today, the company is defending its use of AI and assuring customers that its security safeguards remain intact.

The company’s reputation took a major hit two weeks ago, when 404 Media published a detailed report, based on leaked Slack messages and interviews with anonymous insiders. The story detailed a push by Proton CEO Andy Yen to — in his own words — “ruthlessly smash everything that stands in the way of AI velocity.”

Also: Is your browser’s free VPN enough? I tested 4 against Proton VPN

After one employee proposed spending at least a few days on penetration testing before shipping new code, Yen reportedly said, “That’s bullshit and loser thinking. Of course it’s possible to be fast and secure. Stop thinking always of how to be slower, and focus more on how to be faster.” According to 404 Media’s account, Yen’s message for Proton engineers was to “stop lovingly handcrafting all our code and use AI more.”

After the original story broke, 404 Media posted a follow-up story reporting that the company had canceled its “re-interviews” that were intended to assess how developers were adopting AI. A 404 Media podcast was titled “Proton’s CEO Is Putting AI Before Security.”

That kind of accusation is especially damaging to a company like Proton, whose primary value proposition is privacy and security. The company was founded by engineers at CERN, the Swiss-based physics lab best known for running the Large Hadron Collider (it’s also the birthplace of the World Wide Web). Today, the for-profit Proton AG is primarily owned by the nonprofit Proton Foundation, a structure designed to preserve Proton’s mission to be “private by default,” one that “places people over profits.”

Reddit has an especially active Proton community with 345,000 weekly visitors. It quickly filled up with so many comments — the majority of them negative — that moderators created a single megathread to help keep track of the different threads, including pages for general criticism, support of the AI usage, and “leaving your goodbyes.”

Also: Best VPN services 2026: Expert tested and recommended

Given the level of interest, it’s not surprising that Yen chose that forum to post a public message to the community. In it, he apologized for “hurtful” remarks, adding that he’s “determined to do better.”

He also addressed the topic of AI-based security reviews:

But most importantly, I need to categorically state that our use of AI will never be at the expense of security. In the past year, contrary to what has been alleged, actually additional layers of security review have been added to meet the threat posed by hostile AIs scanning Proton’s open source code for vulnerabilities. Fast and secure need not be mutually exclusive. We also leverage AI to find vulnerabilities in our own code before attackers do. Our code is open source, and its security remains open for all to verify. [emphasis in original]

Nothing in the 404 Media reporting demonstrates that Proton weakened its encryption or shipped insecure code because of AI. The damage is different: Yen’s remarks undermined confidence that management will consistently respect the security processes its customers rely on.

In a blog post that Proton shared with ZDNET ahead of its publication today, the company followed up on Yen’s promise to “lift the hood on how we use AI internally at Proton.” The post, titled “How Proton keeps your data secure in the age of AI,” was signed by the Proton Team and emphasizes Proton’s secure architecture, with end-to-end, zero-access encryption:

We do not hold the keys to your emails, files, or passwords, so we cannot read them, and no AI tool can, either. Our documented security governance policies have not changed since June 2024, before AI was even a major factor in our work here, and they all still apply.

On Reddit, Yen said, “AI is here, and it will unquestionably change how software is developed forever, and Proton must also change.” Today’s post clarified that code receives the same checks whether it is written by a human, by an AI agent, or by a combination of both. Every change receives automated security scans but also requires human approval; sensitive changes, such as core cryptography work, require review by specialist teams. Major feature designs are also reviewed for security, dependencies, testing, and deployment plans.

Also: I can access my home PCs remotely for free – no VPN needed

Those policies address the most serious technical concerns raised by the current controversy, although outsiders have no way to know from the policy alone how consistently those safeguards are followed in day-to-day development.

Should a modern software company use AI for security? Proton argues that it can’t afford not to.

Because of AI, attacks that once might have required a nation-state can now be executed by almost anyone. A defender relying on manual processes and hand-crafted tools is bringing a knife to a gunfight. An attacker only needs to find one flaw, while a defender needs to close all of them. AI is the only tool that makes that defense possible. Cybersecurity is an arms race, and the teams working on security at Proton have been some of the earliest adopters of AI, because the safety of your data depends on winning that race.

But that argument doesn’t really address the objection. The current controversy isn’t about whether Proton should use AI to find vulnerabilities; it’s about whether pressure for “AI velocity” can override the judgment of engineers responsible for keeping its products secure.

Proton says it uses AI to review code changes, identify vulnerabilities through continuous security reviews, and monitor an attack surface that has “grown far beyond what unaided humans could monitor.” The company uses its own models alongside non-public cyber models from OpenAI and Anthropic.

The modern software development landscape is torn by two opposing forces. On one side, you have AI-based tools that are routinely finding nightmarish vulnerabilities in existing codebases and then exploiting them immediately. On the other side, you have a global movement that’s skeptical of the benefits of AI and outright opposed to construction of the data centers where they run.

Much of Proton’s growth over the past few years has been the direct result of people trying to escape big tech. So it shouldn’t be a surprise that many of Proton’s customers — at least those who are passionate enough to visit a Reddit forum — are deeply skeptical of anything related to AI.

The technology may be useful or even essential to shoring up security, but a significant share of the privacy-oriented audience Proton has cultivated is predisposed to distrust AI companies and AI-driven development.

Also: Are free VPNs legit? I asked security experts to learn the true cost

In a traditional software company, that realization would have led management to cautiously roll out AI-based features and reveal its AI-centric security strategy more deliberately. That didn’t happen here, obviously. Those blunt remarks from the CEO exposed a management culture that many employees and customers found troubling. It’s difficult to restore trust, both within the workforce and among customers, after that kind of public display of contempt.

Ironically, one of Yen’s comments from the original 404 Media story describes the company’s current dilemma with devastating accuracy.

In a Slack message, Yen told a Proton engineer, “perception is more important than truth, the truth doesn’t matter if you don’t win on perception.” The implication was that he was urging the employee to lie, or at least shade the truth. In fact, as Yen himself told a random Reddit commenter:

I do not believe perception should be more important than truth. I am stating that unfortunately it often ends up being the case. If you place it into the context in which it was said, it can be understood as, “it doesn’t matter that the truth is that Proton’s PQC implementation is better, this truth is irrelevant as long as it is not perceived to be this way, and we therefore need to correct that perception”

That’s Proton’s challenge now, distilled to its essence. It takes seconds to destroy a reputation. It can take months or years to rebuild the perception that the company and its management can no longer be trusted.

For people who are deeply concerned with privacy, Proton is still a viable choice. Its architecture is solid and its development staff, at least for now, is intact. The blowback from the current controversy has focused management’s attention on the things that matter. And most alternatives, like Tuta and Mailfence, are small and still untested.

Personally, I will continue to use Proton Pass and Proton VPN, both of which are solid, reliable products. But I’ve moved my secondary email accounts (which I had migrated from Gmail to Proton Mail); they’re now running on Fastmail. I had been considering making the switch for a long time, primarily because Proton Mail’s performance has been too slow for my taste. Current events just hastened that switch.

Why homebuilders need business plans that test scenarios

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Scott Cox explains why forcing long-range plans to match annual budgets shifts focus to deadlines over analysis. He recommends a 1-page output and a scenario-ready model leaders can adjust without analyst help.  AI Summary

[In the fourth of seven excerpts from ‘Less Wrong, More Right,’ Scott Cox argues argues a 5-year business plan should be a thinking tool, not a control document. He shares two tests: keep key output to one page and use a single assumptions page for scenario changes.]

Sometimes I think the most misunderstood part of our business is the business plan.

The real purpose is to learn about your business and understand the consequences of the choices available to you. If we go in this direction, what does it mean for our capitalization and our risk? How many people will we need, and when? Is our subcontractor base capable of that volume? For this to pay off, how long does the market have to hold? A plan exists to force that thinking. Everything else it does — satisfying the bank, setting the bonus targets, giving the board something to approve — is a byproduct, and the byproducts have a way of eating the main course.

I sat in a business plan meeting for the division of a multi-region builder. A number of organizational things were in flux, the market was slipping, and to be fair to the local team, they were being asked to work on too many initiatives at once. While everyone debated next year’s goals, I was looking at year five. I did a simple return-on-assets calculation on the page in front of me. It was terrible. I asked whether that was really what they expected, because if that was the plan, I could not support doing any new deals at all. The return on capital did not justify the investment.

After what I will politely describe as an agitated discussion, we got down to the why — the size of the deals, the type of deals, the organizational structure, the capital those choices required. And that should have become the start of a worthwhile conversation. Next year’s results were, within limits, going to be what they were going to be. The bigger issues were the ones worth the room’s time.

Then the deadline arrived. Divisional plans had to be consolidated. The banks and others were waiting. Some minor tweaks were made and the plan went out substantially as drafted. I doubt anyone looked past year one in that document ever again.

That is the failure I want to dwell on, because it was not a failure of intelligence or effort. It was a process that answered to form and deadline instead of thought — a date to hit, a box to check — and once those became the real assignment, the thinking we’d just started in that room stopped mattering.

What we should have been able to do, right there, was stop the meeting and start putting different approaches into the model. Bigger deals, fewer of them. Entitled land versus unentitled. Different timing. Then look at what came out — capital required, when it came back, what the returns actually were. We could not, so we did not. The model was capable of producing one future in enormous detail and incapable of producing a second one at all.

Underneath all of this is an instinct I want to focus on, because it is the one doing the damage. People believe everything should foot. The five-year plan should tie to the annual budget, which should tie to the phase budgets, which should tie to the cash flow. It feels like discipline. It is not.

Budgets are control instruments. Plans are thinking instruments. A control instrument has to foot — that is its job. A six-month cash flow projection cannot be materially wrong without serious consequences. An annual budget is an accountability document and people are going to be held to it. A phase budget for a project you are about to start is a commitment on money you are about to spend. All of those demand precision.

A five-year company plan does not. It is for asking what happens if we run this business differently? When you force a thinking instrument to reconcile with a control instrument, the thinking instrument always loses — because the deadline always belongs to the control instrument. That is not a hypothetical. That is exactly what happened in the meeting I just described.

The first is the one-page test, and it is a test of the output. If what you circulate, at least the important parts, does not fit on an 11×17, you have included things that do not matter. That is partly a communication discipline — that is how a lot of people are actually going to read it — but it is mostly a forcing function. Something has to be left off, and deciding what gets left off is the same as deciding what matters.

The second is a test of the design of the model, and it is the one I care about more. Can the CEO change the assumptions herself, in the room, in real time, to test an idea — without the analyst who built the model sitting next to her? Can I take it home, pour a bourbon after dinner, and try three scenarios on my own? If the answer is yes, the model is simple enough. If the answer is no, you do not have a tool. You have a document.

Back-up tabs are fine. Detail underneath is fine. The requirement is that a single, clearly marked assumptions page drives all of it with comprehensible results, and that changing one thing changes everything downstream without anyone opening the hood.

The principle underneath is that granularity should be set by what you need to move, not by what the budget happens to contain.

The 11×17 should just be a bigger, prettier version of the yellow pad. If it is anything more than that, ask yourself what all the rest of it is actually for.

Adapted from Less Wrong, More Right (Part III: Running the Business), available on Amazon.

LIVE – Will CPI Cause a Sell-Off? Asian Stocks and Bitcoin Face Fresh Test as NVIDIA Anxiety Grows

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Ahmed Barakat is a journalist and copywriter based in Georgia with a growing focus on blockchain technology, DeFi, AI, privacy, digital assets, and fintech innovation.

The CryptoNews editorial team is composed of seasoned writers specializing in cryptocurrency and blockchain technology. Their expertise ensures comprehensive, accurate, and insightful content for...

Will CPI cause a sell-off across Asian stocks and Bitcoin? Asian stocks staged a sharp recovery this morning, but another US inflation report could put the rebound to the test. Investors are preparing for October’s Consumer Price Index (CPI), with expectations of slower monthly inflation offering some relief. However, rising Federal Reserve rate-hike bets and NVIDIA shareholders suing Jensen Huang have kept markets cautious.

The Korea’s KOSPI jumped 4.5% this morning trading, led by Samsung Electronics and SK Hynix. Hong Kong’s Hang Seng Index also advanced, while Singapore’s Straits Times Index joined the regional recovery. Bitcoin, meanwhile, remains below $75,000 ahead of the US inflation release.

South Korean stocks getting sent 📈 🤑 🥳 pic.twitter.com/KYd5V9Oh9d

Does this recovery have enough momentum to survive another inflation surprise? A hotter reading could revive concerns about borrowing costs, while weaker inflation might give risk assets room to recover. Will the latest CPI figures determine if the rebound continues, or will it cause a sell-off?

Discover: The Crypto Presales This Q4 2026

The US Bureau of Labor Statistics is scheduled to publish October CPI at 8:30 a.m. ET on November 10. Economists expect headline inflation to increase 0.3% month-over-month, slowing from September’s 0.6% rise. Core CPI, which excludes food and energy, is forecast to increase 0.2%, compared with 0.3% previously.

Last year’s government shutdown prevented the BLS from collecting the necessary October 2025 data. Economists nevertheless estimate headline inflation would have reached 3.9% annually.

The stakes remain high because markets have increasingly priced in another Federal Reserve rate hike. Traders recently assigned an 84% to 85% probability to a December increase, following stronger employment data. The Fed’s benchmark interest rate currently stands at 3.75% to 4%.

A hotter-than-expected reading could strengthen the case for tighter monetary policy. Higher Treasury yields and a stronger dollar would potentially pressure Asian equities, particularly technology companies with valuations sensitive to financing costs.

South Korea’s KOSPI could face the greatest exposure. Samsung Electronics and SK Hynix are central to the global semiconductor supply chain, making the index particularly sensitive to changes in AI investment sentiment. Both companies helped drive today’s rebound, with their shares rising 5.1% and 6.8%, respectively.

Hong Kong’s Hang Seng Index offers another important benchmark. Its technology-heavy components could face renewed pressure if investors reduce exposure to growth stocks. Singapore’s Straits Times Index provides a useful contrast, given its greater exposure to financial institutions and dividend-paying companies.

A cooler reading could produce the opposite reaction, easing rate concerns and supporting another regional advance.

Bitcoin is trading around $74,600 ahead of the inflation release, leaving it close to a crucial support zone. Our analysts identified the $73,000 level as an important level, with a sustained break potentially exposing $70,000.

A hotter CPI reading could intensify selling pressure as investors reassess interest-rate expectations. Bitcoin’s recent weakness also reflects fading institutional demand, with US spot Bitcoin exchange-traded funds recording $1.28 billion in outflows last week.

A softer inflation report could trigger a relief rally, but it might not guarantee a sustained recovery. Stronger ETF demand and improved liquidity would still be necessary to support a meaningful reversal.

NVIDIA adds another complication. The chipmaker and CEO Jensen Huang is being sued in a proposed shareholder class action filed in California yesterday. Investor Mark Kistenmacher alleges that NVIDIA misled shareholders about Huang’s relationship with Alice Huang, a former executive at Singapore-based data center company Megaspeed.

BREAKING: Nvidia, $NVDA, and Jensen Huang are being sued in a class action lawsuit, accusing ‌them of defrauding shareholders by concealing Huang's ties with a suspected Chinese spy.The lawsuit claims that shareholders were misled about Huang's alleged relationship with a woman…

The complaint claims the alleged relationship created reputational and national security risks that investors should have known about. NVIDIA’s board had already announced an independent investigation, while the company had not immediately commented on the lawsuit.

NVIDIA shares fell 4.3% on Monday, erasing $190 billion in market cap. The decline arrived just as Asian semiconductor shares were attempting to recover from last week’s technology-led sell-off.

NVIDIA remains central to the AI investment cycle, and any deterioration in confidence surrounding the company could amplify concerns about semiconductor valuations and the sustainability of AI spending. That could leave Korean chipmakers particularly vulnerable if another inflation shock hits markets.

So, will CPI cause a sell-off? It depends on a few factors. We might need to watch core prices, Treasury yields, and the dollar for clues about the Federal Reserve’s next move. A cooler report could extend the recovery, but a hotter reading may expose how fragile confidence in Asian technology stocks and Bitcoin is.
